A Liouville Theorem and C α C^{\alpha} -Estimate for Calabi-Yau Cones
Johan Jacoby Klemmensen

Abstract
. Let ( 𝒞 , ω 𝒞 ) (\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}}) be a Ricci-flat, simply connected, conical Kähler manifold. We establish a Liouville theorem for constant scalar curvature Kähler (cscK) metrics on 𝒞 \mathscr{C} . The theorem asserts that any cscK metric ω \omega satisfying the uniform bound 1 C ​ ω 𝒞 ⩽ ω ⩽ C ​ ω 𝒞 \frac{1}{C}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}\leqslant\omega\leqslant C\omega_{\mathscr{C}} for some C ⩾ 1 C\geqslant 1 is equal to ω 𝒞 \omega_{\mathscr{C}} up to a holomorphic automorphism that commutes with the scaling action of the cone structure. Next, we develop a C 0 , α C^{0,\alpha} -estimate for uniformly bounded Kähler metrics on a ball around the apex, using a Hölder-type seminorm inspired by Krylov. This estimate applies for small α > 0 \alpha>0 under the assumption of uniformly bounded scalar curvature. As a corollary of this result, we show that such a Kähler metric ω \omega is asymptotic to the Ricci-flat cone metric ω 𝒞 \omega_{\mathscr{C}} , with polynomial decay rate r α r^{\alpha} and for sufficiently small α > 0 \alpha>0 .



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Background and Motivation

One of the first use cases of Liouville Theorems and scaling arguments to prove Schauder estimates goes back to the work of Simon
[
46
]
in his work on linear elliptic operators on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Later, similar results were developed for Kähler metrics satisfying the complex Monge-Ampère equation. In this context, the classical Liouville Theorem for Kähler metrics on
\mathbb{C}^{m}
dates back to Riebesehl-Schulz
[
42
]
, which states that any Kähler metric with a constant determinant and uniformly equivalent to the Euclidean metric, is the pullback of the Euclidean metric by an element in
\GL(m,\mathbb{C})
. Utilizing their results and rescaling methods, Chen-Wang provided a new proof
[
11
]
of the
C^{2,\alpha}
-estimate for solutions of the complex Monge-Ampère equation on Euclidean space. A similar statement for Kähler metrics on
\mathbb{C}^{m}
with singularities along a divisor was given in
[
13
]
by Chen-Wang, with the Liouville Theorem stemming from a strengthening of the Liouville Theorems found in
[
10
]
and
[
12
]
. To prove
C^{k,\alpha}
-estimates on product Kähler manifolds with collapsing fibers, more elaborate scaling arguments were presented in
[
29
]
by Hein-Tosatti. The necessary Liouville Theorem is found in
[
25
]
by Hein, with a simplified proof by Li-Li-Zhang
[
34
]
.

Tangentially to this, Hein-Sun proved
[
28
]
the following result: Given a complex projective variety such that the singularities are isolated and locally isomorphic to Calabi-Yau cones, then, if these cones are in addition smoothable and strongly regular, any Ricci-flat Kähler metric on the variety asymptotically approaches the given Ricci-flat Kähler cone metric. This work was later extended by Chiu-Székelyhidi in
[
15
]
to the case of a tangent cone that is not necessarily locally isomorphic to the germ of the singularity and with a non-smooth cross-section, thus in particular dropping the strongly regular but not the smoothable assumption.

This paper shows that, on any simply-connected Calabi-Yau cone with a smooth cross-section, polynomial convergence follows from uniformly bounded scalar curvature and uniform equivalence to the cone metric. For
\alpha>0
small enough, then any such
\omega
is asymptotic to
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
at polynomial rate
r^{\alpha}
and up to some holomorphic automorphism
\Psi
commuting with scaling. Smoothability of the cone and Donaldson-Sun theory
[
19
]
are not needed for this. In a sense, these assumptions get replaced by the uniform equivalence, and we then prove an Evans-Krylov estimate, which ultimately implies polynomial convergence at the apex as a corollary.
The proof proceeds using the scaling methods presented in
[
29
]
, where the proof relies on blowup arguments and applying Liouville theorems. The quintessential Liouville Theorem for this argument is Theorem
A
, which describes the global behavior of constant scalar curvature Kähler (cscK) metrics on Calabi-Yau conical manifolds
\mathscr{C}
(see Definition
2.1
).

1.2.
Structure of the Paper

We present the following Liouville Theorem for cscK metrics on Ricci-flat Kähler/Calabi-Yau cones
(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
. For
\mathscr{C}=\mathbb{C}^{m}\setminus\{0\}
, the proof presented is novel and independent of
[
42
]
and
[
34
]
, where Ricci-flatness instead of cscK is also required. Let
\Aut_{\Scl}(\mathscr{C})
denote the group of holomorphic automorphisms commuting with the action of the scaling vector field.

Theorem A

(Theorem
2.3
)
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
be a Calabi-Yau cone. Take any cscK metric
\omega
on
\mathscr{C}
such that there exists some constant
C>0
with

\frac{1}{C}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}\leqslant\omega\leqslant C\omega_{\mathscr{C}}.

Then
\omega=\Psi^{*}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
for some cone automorphism
\Psi\in\Aut_{\Scl}(\mathscr{C})
.

The outline of the proof is as follows: By rescaling, the basic theory of pluriharmonic functions and a Harnack inequality in a neighborhood of the apex show that
\omega^{m}=\omega_{\mathscr{C}}^{m}
. Section
2.2
uses heat kernel estimates to show that the asymptotic limits of
\omega
at
o
and
\infty
are Riemannian cone metrics, henceforth called the tangent cones. The tangent cone metrics have associated Reeb fields, and the classification by Hein-Sun
[
28
]
of holomorphic vector fields commuting with
r\partial_{r}
restricts the choice of Reeb fields. This is presented in Section
2.3
. Section
2.4
uses the work of Martelli-Sparks-Yau
[
35
]
to conclude that the tangent cones have the same Reeb field as
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
. Applying the arguments of Bando-Mabuchi
[
4
]
, suitably generalized by Nitta-Sekiya
[
39
]
, proves in Section
2.4
that the tangent cones and
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
are related by an automorphism in
\Aut_{\Scl}(\mathscr{C})
. This is exactly the statement of Theorem
A
applied to the tangent cones. Finally, Section
2.5
reapplies the heat kernel estimates from Section
2.2
to
\omega
to prove the Liouville Theorem in full generality.

In Section
3
, a novel
C^{0,\alpha}
-type seminorm is presented and used to prove a
C^{0,\alpha}
-estimate of another Kähler metric at the apex
o
.

Theorem B

(Theorem
3.15
)
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
be a Calabi-Yau cone with cone metric
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
, and let
\omega
be a Kähler metric on
B_{3}(o)\subset\mathscr{C}
such that

(1.1)

\frac{1}{C}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}\leqslant\omega\leqslant C\omega_{\mathscr{C}},\quad\left\lVert\Scal(\omega)\right\rVert_{0,B_{3}(o)}\leqslant D,

for some constants
C,D>0
. Then for any
\alpha=\alpha(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})>0
small enough, there exists a constant
C^{\prime}
with

[\omega]^{\prime}_{\alpha,B_{1}(o),\Sigma_{3C}^{2}\times\Sigma_{\loc}^{2}}\leqslant C^{\prime},

where
\alpha=\alpha(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
and
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime}(\mathscr{C},C,D,\alpha,\omega_{\mathscr{C}},\mathfrak{U})
are independent of
\omega
.

A similar estimate for the Laplacian acting on functions can be found in Proposition
3.5
.
\mathfrak{U}
is a choice of covering given in Section
3.1
. The
L^{\infty}
-scalar curvature bound ensures that a blowup of
\omega
at the apex is a cscK metric, allowing us to use Theorem
A
in the proof of Theorem
B
.

The definition of
[\omega]^{\prime}_{\alpha,B_{1}(o),\Sigma_{3C}^{2}\times\Sigma_{\loc}^{2}}
can be found in Definition
3.3
. It aims to measure the weighted distance between
\omega
and the comparison set
\Sigma_{3C}^{2}\times\Sigma_{\loc}^{2}
in the
C^{0}
-norm.
\Sigma_{3C}^{2}
contains pullbacks of the cone metric
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
by holomorphic automorphisms commuting with scaling.
\Sigma_{\loc}^{2}
contains notions of constant 2-form that are only locally defined, and
\mathfrak{U}
is a set of open sets where elements in
\Sigma_{\loc}^{2}
are defined.. If
\mathscr{C}\cup\{o\}=\mathbb{C}^{m}
and the comparison set consists of constant 2-forms, the seminorm is equivalent to the usual
C^{0,\alpha}
-seminorm (see
[
32
, Theorem 3.3.1]
). To prove the theorem, we follow the ideas outlined in
[
29
]
by first assuming that
[\omega_{i}]^{\prime}_{\alpha,B_{1}(o),\Sigma_{3C}^{2}\times\Sigma_{\loc}^{2}}
is unbounded for some sequence
(\omega_{i})
, and then blowing up the space such that
[\tilde{\omega}_{i}]^{\prime}_{\alpha,B_{(\epsilon_{i}^{-1})}(o),\Sigma_{3C}^{2}\times\Sigma_{\loc}^{2}}=1
for the blow-up sequence
(\tilde{\omega}_{i})
with
\epsilon_{i}\to 0
. We select points
x_{i}\in\mathscr{C}\cup\{o\}
and radii
\varrho_{i}>0
to maximize the seminorm for each
i
. Depending on whether
\varrho_{i}\to 0,\varrho_{i}\to\infty
, or
\varrho_{i}
remains bounded away from these values, we apply different Liouville theorems (including Theorem
A
) to obtain a contradiction. The proof is found in Section
3
.

An application of Theorem
B
gives the asymptotic behavior of such Kähler metrics near the apex. The corollary also proves that the tangent cone at
o
of any such
\omega
is unique.

Corollary C

(Corollary
3.16
)
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
be a Calabi-Yau cone with cone metric
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
, and let
\omega
be a Kähler metric on
B_{3}(o)\subset\mathscr{C}
such that

\frac{1}{C}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}\leqslant\omega\leqslant C\omega_{\mathscr{C}},\quad\left\lVert\Scal(\omega)\right\rVert_{0,B_{3}(o)}\leqslant D,

for some constants
C,D>0
. Then, for any
\alpha=\alpha(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})>0
small enough, there exists an automorphism
\Psi\in\Aut_{\Scl}(\mathscr{C})
with

|\Psi^{*}\omega-\omega_{\mathscr{C}}|_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}\leqslant C^{\prime}r^{\alpha}

for
r\leqslant 1
.
\alpha=\alpha(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
and
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime}(\mathscr{C},C,D,\alpha,\omega_{\mathscr{C}},\mathfrak{U})
are independent of
\omega
.

If
\Scal(\omega)=0
, then

|\nabla_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}^{k}(\Psi^{*}\omega)-\omega_{\mathscr{C}}|_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}\leqslant C_{k}^{\prime}r^{\alpha-k},\qquad k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},

for
r\leqslant 1
and
C_{k}^{\prime}=C_{k}^{\prime}(\mathscr{C},C,D,k,\alpha,\omega_{\mathscr{C}},\mathfrak{U})
.

2.
A Liouville Theorem for Calabi-Yau Cones

This section proves a Liouville theorem for Calabi-Yau cones by demonstrating that any cscK metric uniformly equivalent to the cone metric is equal to the cone metric up to a biholomorphism commuting with scaling. First, Calabi-Yau cones are defined as follows:

Definition 2.1
.

•

Let
(\mathscr{C},J,g_{\mathscr{C}})
be a complex
m
-dimensional Kähler manifold with complex structure
J
, metric
g_{\mathscr{C}}
, and associated
(1,1)
-form (or Kähler form)
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
. We will often not distinguish between a metric and its Kähler form. The pair
(\mathscr{C},g_{\mathscr{C}})
(also written
(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
) is a Kähler cone if there exists a diffeomorphism
\mathscr{C}\simeq\mathbb{R}_{+}\times L
with
\mathbb{R}_{+}\coloneqq(0,\infty)
and for some closed and connected
(2m-1)
-dimensional manifold
L
, called the link. Furthermore, on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times L
,
g_{\mathscr{C}}
takes the form
g_{\mathscr{C}}=dr^{2}+r^{2}h
with
r
the coordinate on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
h
a metric on the link
L
. The completion of the cone
\mathscr{C}
by including the apex
o
is denoted by
\overline{\mathscr{C}}\coloneqq\mathscr{C}\cup\{o\}
.

•

A Kähler cone
(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
is a Calabi-Yau cone if
\mathscr{C}
is simply-connected and the associated Kähler metric
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
is Ricci-flat.

•

We denote by
\Aut_{\Scl}(\mathscr{C})
the holomorphic automorphisms of
\mathscr{C}
commuting with scaling. Equivalently,
\Aut_{\Scl}(\mathscr{C})
are the holomorphic automorphisms preserving the scaling vector field
r\partial_{r}
(and thereby also the Reeb field
\xi=Jr\partial_{r}
). The action extends to
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
by fixing
o\in\overline{\mathscr{C}}
.

•

All constants in the estimates below may change from line to line.

Notation 2.2
.

We always assume that the pair
(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
is Calabi-Yau.

As
(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
is a Kähler cone, the pair
(L,h)
forms a Sasaki structure. Ricci-flatness implies that
(L,h)
is Sasaki-Einstein with an Einstein constant of
2m-2
. The vector field
r\partial_{r}
is called the scaling vector field of
({\mathscr{C}},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
and the cone metric
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
satisfies
\mathscr{L}_{r\partial_{r}}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}=2\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
. The Reeb field
\xi\coloneqq J(r\partial_{r})
is the image of the scaling vector field
r\partial_{r}
under the complex structure
J
. Furthermore, the Reeb field
\xi
restricts to
L
and generates a torus action on this space. Using this setup, we prove the following theorem:

Theorem 2.3

(Liouville Theorem for Calabi-Yau cones)
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
be a Calabi-Yau cone, and take a cscK metric
\omega
on
\mathscr{C}
such that there exists some constant
C>0
with

(2.1)

\frac{1}{C}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}\leqslant\omega\leqslant C\omega_{\mathscr{C}}.

Then
\omega=\Psi^{*}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
for some cone automorphism
\Psi\in\Aut_{\Scl}(\mathscr{C})
.

Notation 2.4
.

•

Throughout the paper, all balls are measured with respect to
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
, i.e. for
p\in{\mathscr{C}}
and
r>0
,
B_{r}(p)=\{x\in{\mathscr{C}}\mid\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(p,x)<r\}
is the open ball with center
p
and radius
r
with respect to
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
.

We extend the balls to measure distances from the apex. Let
o\in\overline{\mathscr{C}}
be the apex, and take
r>0
.
\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}
extends from
{\mathscr{C}}
to a unique distance metric on
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
, also denoted by
\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}
. Then

B_{r}(o)\coloneqq\{x\in{\mathscr{C}}\mid\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(o,x)<r\}.

Notice that
o\notin B_{r}(o)
.

•

The closure
\overline{B_{r}(p)}\subset\overline{\mathscr{C}}
is with respect to
\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}
extended to
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
. Hence, if
\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(0,B_{r}(p))=0
, then
o\in\overline{B_{r}(p)}
.

•

All norms considered in this paper are with respect to
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
unless otherwise specified, i.e.
|\cdot|=|\cdot|_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}
.

•

When convenient, we identify any point
p=(r,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times L
under the identification
\mathscr{C}\cong\mathbb{R}_{+}\times L
.

2.1.
From Scalar Curvature to the Complex Monge-Ampère Equation

This section transforms the cscK condition in the Liouville Theorem into a complex Monge-Ampère equation for
\omega
. In order to accomplish this, we need to review the Hölder norms on manifolds:

Definition 2.5

(
[
29
, Definition 3.1]
)
.

Let
(X,g)
be a Riemannian manifold and
E\to X
a vector bundle on
X
with bundle metric
h
and
h
-preserving connection
\nabla
. If
x,y\in X
and if there is a unique minimal
g
-geodesic
\gamma
joining
y
to
x
, let
\textbf{P}_{xy}
denote
\nabla
-parallel transport on
E
along
\gamma
.
\textbf{P}_{xy}
is undefined if there is no unique minimal geodesic. If
B_{\lambda}(p)
is the
g
-geodesic ball of radius
\lambda
at
p
, define:

[f]_{\alpha,B_{\lambda}(p)}\coloneqq\sup\left\{\frac{|f(x)-\textbf{P}_{xy}(f(y))|_{h(x)}}{d(x,y)^{\alpha}}\mid x\neq y,\textbf{P}_{xy}\text{ is defined}\right\},

for all sections
f\in C^{0,\alpha}_{\loc}(B_{2\lambda}(x),E)
. Here we use the fact that any minimal geodesic in
B_{\lambda}(x)
is contained in
B_{2\lambda}(x)
. If
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{0,B_{\lambda}(p)}
is the usual
C^{0}
-norm with respect to
g
, the full
C^{0,\alpha}
-norm is:

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{0,\alpha,B_{\lambda}(p)}\coloneqq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{0,B_{\lambda}(p)}+[f]_{\alpha,B_{\lambda}(p)}.

The
C^{k,\alpha}
-norm is:

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{k,\alpha,B_{\lambda}(p)}\coloneqq\sum_{l=0}^{k}\left\lVert\nabla^{l}f\right\rVert_{0,B_{\lambda}(p)}+[\nabla^{k}f]_{\alpha,B_{\lambda}(p)}.

We will only consider
E=\mathscr{C}\times\mathbb{C}
or
E=\bigwedge^{l}(\mathscr{C})
, where
h
is induced from the metric
g
.

Lemma 2.6
.

For any two points
x,y\in\mathscr{C}
, there exists a length-minimizing geodesic
\gamma
with
x
and
y
as endpoints such that
\length_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(\gamma)=\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(x,y)
. If
\mathscr{C}=\mathbb{C}^{m}\setminus\{0\}
and
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}=\omega_{\eucl}
, the theorem is only true after adding the smooth point
0\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
.

Proof.

As
\Ric(\omega_{\mathscr{C}})=0
, the link
L
has Ricci curvature
\Ric(\omega_{\mathscr{C}}|_{L})=2m-2
as it is Sasaki-Einstein, implying that

\diam_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(L)\leqslant\pi.

By Bonnet-Myers, if
\diam_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(L)=\pi
, then
L\cong S^{2m-1}
and
(\overline{\mathscr{C}},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})\cong(\mathbb{C}^{m},\omega_{\eucl})
by a rigidity theorem of Cheng
[
14
, Theorem 3.1]
. In this case, the lemma is trivial after adding the apex
0\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
. For
\diam_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(L)<\pi
, the proof is given in
[
5
, Theorem 3.6.17]
.
∎

Remark 2.7
.

On any Ricci-flat Kähler cone
(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
, any two points are connected by a minimizing geodesic (Lemma
2.6
). For any points
p=(r,x),q=(s,y)\in\mathscr{C}\cong\mathbb{R}_{+}\times L
, the minimizing geodesic has the form

\tilde{\gamma}(t)=(r(t),\gamma(t)),

where
\gamma(t)
is a minimizing geodesic in
L
between
x
and
y
. This is unique away from the cut locus of
x
, hence any point
p\in\mathscr{C}
is connected to any
q\in\mathscr{C}
except for a set of Hausdorff dimension at most
2m-1
.

The first step is to prove the following regularity theorem for the complex Monge-Ampère equation.

Proposition 2.8
.

Let
\omega
be a Kähler metric on a ball
B_{6}(0)\subset\mathbb{C}^{m}
. Assume that there exists constants
C,D>0
such that

\frac{1}{C}\omega_{\eucl}\leqslant\omega\leqslant C\omega_{\eucl},\quad\left\lVert\Scal(\omega)\right\rVert_{L^{p}(B_{6}(0))}\leqslant D,

for
p>2m
. Then

\left\lVert\omega\right\rVert_{1,\alpha,B_{1}(0)}\leqslant C_{1}

for
\alpha=1-\frac{2m}{p}
and constants
C_{1}=C_{1}(m,C,D,p)
.

Proof.

If
\omega^{m}=e^{f}\omega_{\eucl}^{m}
, the scalar curvature condition implies that

(2.2)

\left\lVert\Delta_{\omega}f\right\rVert_{L^{p}(B_{6}(0))}\leqslant D.

According to
[
24
, Theorem 4.18]
, there exists a constant
\alpha=\alpha(m,p,C)
such that

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{0,\alpha,B_{5}(0)}\leqslant C_{1}

for
0<\alpha<1
. The
C^{0,\alpha}
-estimate for the complex Monge-Ampère equation
[
11
, Theorem 1.1]
(see also
[
7
]
and
[
43
]
) shows that

(2.3)

\left\lVert\omega\right\rVert_{0,\alpha,B_{4}(0)}\leqslant C_{2}.

Due to the uniform continuity of
\omega
resulting from (
2.3
),
[
20
, Theorem 9.11]
and (
2.2
) imply that

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{W^{2,p}(B_{3}(0))}\leqslant C_{3}.

Morrey’s inequality
[
1
, Theorem 2.30]
. shows that
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{1,\alpha,B_{3}(0)}\leqslant C_{4}
for
\alpha=1-\frac{2m}{p}>0
. By
[
11
, Lemma 2.1]
, there exists a potential
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(B_{2}(0))
such that
i\partial\overline{\partial}\varphi=\omega
and
\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{2,\alpha,B_{2}(0)}\leqslant C\left\lVert\omega\right\rVert_{0,\alpha,B_{3}(0)}
. Differentiating the complex Monge-Ampère equation
\omega^{m}=e^{f}\omega_{\eucl}^{m}
in direction
x_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,2m
, we obtain:

m\omega^{m-1}\wedge i\partial\overline{\partial}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x_{i}}=\frac{\partial e^{f}}{\partial x_{i}}\omega_{\eucl}^{m},

so

(2.4)

m\Delta_{\omega}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x_{i}}=\frac{\partial e^{f}}{\partial x_{i}}\frac{\omega_{\eucl}^{m}}{\omega^{m}},

with the right-hand side uniformly bounded in
C^{0,\alpha}(B_{3}(0))
The Schauder estimates
[
20
, Theorem 9.19]
imply that
\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x_{i}}
is bounded in
C^{2,\alpha}(B_{1}(0))
and hence
\omega
is bounded in
C^{1,\alpha}(B_{1}(0))
.
∎

Corollary 2.9
.

Let
\omega
be a Kähler metric on a ball
B_{2}(0)\subset\mathbb{C}^{m}
. Assume that there exists constants
C,D>0
such that

\frac{1}{C}\omega_{\eucl}\leqslant\omega\leqslant C\omega_{\eucl},\quad\left\lVert\Scal(\omega)\right\rVert_{0,B_{2}(0)}\leqslant D,

For any function
f\colon C^{2}(B_{\frac{3}{2}}(0))\to\mathbb{R}
and
\alpha\in(0,1)
, there exists a constant
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime}(m,C,D,\alpha)>0
such that

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{1,\alpha,B_{1}(0)}\leqslant C^{\prime}(\left\lVert\Delta_{\omega}f\right\rVert_{0,B_{\frac{3}{2}}(0)}+\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{0,B_{\frac{3}{2}}(0)}).

Proof.

After rescaling, Lemma
2.8
shows that
\omega
is uniformly bounded in
C^{1,\alpha}(B_{\frac{3}{2}}(0))
for all
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Using the
W^{2,p}
-estimates for the Laplacian for
p\in(1,\infty)
, then

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{W^{2,p}(B_{\frac{5}{4}}(0))}\leqslant C^{\prime}(\left\lVert\Delta_{\omega}f\right\rVert_{L^{p}(B_{\frac{3}{2}}(0))}+\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{p}(B_{\frac{3}{2}}(0)}))\leqslant C^{\prime}(\left\lVert\Delta_{\omega}f\right\rVert_{0,B_{\frac{3}{2}}(0)}+\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{0,B_{\frac{3}{2}}(0)}).

Morrey’s inequality
[
1
, Theorem 2.30]
shows that

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{1,1-\frac{2m}{p},B_{1}(0)}\leqslant C^{\prime}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{W^{2,p}(B_{\frac{5}{4}}(0))}

for
p>2m
, finishing the proof as
p\in(2m,\infty)
can be chosen arbitrarily.
∎

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
\omega
be a Kähler metric on
\mathscr{C}
satisfying (
2.1
) and with scalar curvature
\Scal(\omega)
bounded in
C^{0}(\mathscr{C}\setminus B_{R}(o))
for some radius
R>0
. Let
\varphi\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathbb{R}
be a bounded function such that

\Delta_{\omega}\varphi=A

for some constant
A\in\mathbb{R}
. Then
A=0
and
\varphi
is a constant.

If
A=0
is given, the requirement
\Scal(\omega)
bounded in
C^{0}(\mathscr{C}\setminus B_{R}(o))
is not necessary.

Proof.

∎

Proof.

Assume that
A\neq 0
and, without loss of generality, assume
A>0
by changing the sign of
\varphi
if necessary. We will now show that
A\neq 0
is not possible. Proposition
2.8
, applied to balls
B_{1}(p)\subset\mathscr{C}\setminus B_{R}(o)
, shows that
\omega
is bounded in
C^{1,\alpha}_{\loc}(\mathscr{C}\setminus B_{2R}(o))
for any
0<\alpha<1
. The Schauder estimates
[
20
, Theorem 9.19]
, applied to
\varphi
, imply that
\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{C^{3,\alpha}_{\loc}(\mathscr{C}\setminus B_{2R}(o))}\leqslant C
. The estimates are uniform away from the apex on balls of fixed radii. Therefore, by selecting points
p_{i}\in\mathscr{C}
with
\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(o,p_{i})\to\infty
and increasing balls
B_{r_{i}}(p_{i})
with
r_{i}\to\infty
not growing too fast compared to
\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(o,p_{i})
, we obtain a
C^{1,\alpha}_{\loc}
-sublimit
\omega|_{B_{r_{i}}(p_{i})}\to\omega_{\infty}
and a
C^{3,\alpha}_{\loc}
-sublimit
\varphi|_{B_{r_{i}}(p_{i})}\to\varphi_{\infty}\in C_{\loc}^{3,\alpha}(\mathbb{C}^{m})
satisfying

\frac{1}{C}\omega_{\eucl}\leqslant\omega_{\infty}\leqslant C\omega_{\eucl},\quad\left\lVert\omega_{\infty}\right\rVert_{0,\alpha,\mathbb{C}^{m}}\leqslant D,\quad\Delta_{\omega_{\infty}}\varphi_{\infty}=A.

To obtain a contradiction with
A\neq 0
, consider

(2.5)

\Delta_{\omega_{\infty}}\varphi_{\infty}=i\tr_{\omega_{\infty}}(\partial\overline{\partial}\varphi_{\infty})=i\tr(\omega_{\infty}^{-1}\partial\overline{\partial}\varphi_{\infty})=i\tr(U\omega_{\infty}^{-1}U^{*}U(\partial\overline{\partial}\varphi_{\infty})U^{*})=A,

where
U=\SU(m)
is a matrix such that
U\omega_{\infty}^{-1}U^{*}
is diagonal at
0\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
. By the uniform bound (
2.1
), the eigenvalues are bounded from below by
\frac{1}{C}
and from above by
C
, i.e.

iU\omega_{\infty}(0)^{-1}U^{*}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{m}),\quad\frac{1}{C}\leqslant a_{i}\leqslant C\quad\text{for all }i=1,\dots,m.

Since
U
defines a complex change of coordinates,
U(\partial\overline{\partial}\varphi_{\infty})U^{*}
represents the complex Hessian of
\varphi_{\infty}
in the new coordinates defined by
U
, denoted by
(z_{1}=x_{1}+iy_{1},\dots,z_{m}=x_{m}+iy_{m})
. Let
\partial_{i}=\partial_{z_{i}}
be the
\partial
-operator in the direction of
z_{i}
, and let
\partial_{\overline{i}}
be the
\overline{\partial}
-operator in the direction of
\overline{z_{i}}
. Since
U\omega_{\infty}(0)^{-1}U^{*}
is diagonal, (
2.5
) evaluates to

\Delta_{\omega_{\infty}}\varphi_{\infty}=i\tr(U\omega_{\infty}^{-1}U^{*}U(\partial\overline{\partial}\varphi_{\infty})U^{*})=i\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}\partial_{i}\partial_{\overline{i}}\varphi_{\infty}=A>0.

For all
a_{i}
,
C\geqslant a_{i}\geqslant\frac{1}{C}
, so there is a direction
z_{i}
such that
i\partial_{i}\partial_{\overline{i}}\varphi_{\infty}=(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{i}^{2}}+\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial y_{i}^{2}})\varphi_{\infty}\geqslant\frac{A}{Cm}
. Therefore, either
\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{i}^{2}}\varphi_{\infty}\geqslant\frac{A}{2Cm}
or
\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial y_{i}^{2}}\varphi_{\infty}\geqslant\frac{A}{2Cm}
. Assuming that
\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{i}^{2}}\varphi_{\infty}\geqslant\frac{A}{2Cm}
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\varphi_{\infty}\geqslant 0
(otherwise consider
-\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\varphi_{\infty}
), the idea is to integrate along
x_{i}
as long as
\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{i}^{2}}\varphi_{\infty}\geqslant\frac{A}{4Cm}
. The uniform
C^{3,\alpha}_{\loc}
-estimate on
\varphi_{\infty}
guarantees that there always exists a uniform minimum length
l_{\min}
for which this is true. Let
p_{\min}
be the point reached after moving along
x_{i}
for
l_{\min}
units. Integrate along this path to find

\varphi_{\infty}(p_{\min})-\varphi_{\infty}(0)\geqslant\frac{A}{8Cm}l_{\min}^{2}.

Starting from
p_{\min}
, the same procedure can be iterated as many times as needed to show that, for all
b>0
, there is a point
p\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
such that

\varphi_{\infty}(p)-\varphi_{\infty}(0)>b.

This contradicts
\varphi_{\infty}
being a bounded function and so
\Delta_{\omega}\varphi=0
.

Assume now that
\Delta_{\omega}\varphi=0
on
(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
. As we will see in the proof of Theorem
2.16
for the heat kernel, one can prove a Harnack inequality for the heat equation by adopting the standard proof for parabolic differential equations on domains
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
to neighborhoods
B_{r}(o)\subset\mathscr{C}
. The Harnack inequality only requires the uniform bound (
2.1
) on
\omega
. Extend
\varphi
constantly along
\mathbb{R}
to the domain
\mathbb{R}\times\mathscr{C}
such that it solves the heat equation:

(\partial_{t}-\Delta_{\omega})\varphi=0,

since
\varphi
and
\omega
are invariant in
t
on the manifold
\mathbb{R}\times\mathscr{C}
. Setting
t=0
we obtain by (
2.16
):

(2.6)

|\varphi(x)-\varphi(y)|\leqslant E\left(\frac{\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(x,y)}{r}\right)^{\gamma}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}([-r^{2},0]\times B_{r}(o))},

for all
r<\infty
, some
\gamma,\delta>0
, all points
x,y\in B_{\delta r}(o)
, and some constant
E=E(\mathscr{C},\gamma,m,\delta)>0
. Taking
r\to\infty
, it follows that
\varphi(x)-\varphi(y)=0
for all
x,y\in\mathscr{C}
and so
\varphi
is a constant.
∎

Corollary 2.11
.

Any metric
\omega
from Theorem
2.3
satisfies

(2.7)

\omega^{m}=\omega_{\mathscr{C}}^{m}

after rescaling. In particular,
\omega
is Ricci-flat.

Proof.

Take
\omega
as in Theorem
2.3
and write
\omega^{m}=e^{F}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}^{m}
. The uniform bound (
2.1
) implies that
F
is bounded, and constant scalar curvature
\Scal(\omega)=A\in\mathbb{R}
is equivalent to

(2.8)

-\Delta_{\omega}F=A.

By Proposition
2.10
, the function
F
is constant and
A=0
. Therefore,
\omega
satisfies (
2.7
) after rescaling and is, in particular, Ricci-flat.
∎

By Corollary
2.11
we assume for the remainder of the section that
\omega
satisfies (
2.7
).

2.2.
A Liouville Theorem for the Tangent Cones at
\infty
and
o

2.2.1.
Cone Structure of the Asymptotic Limits

Denote by
\Phi_{\lambda}\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}
the rescaling
(r,x)\mapsto(\lambda r,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times L
. Take a Kähler metric
\omega
satisfying the properties of Theorem
2.3
. Form the sequence of blow-down metrics

(2.9)

\omega_{\epsilon_{i}}=\Phi^{*}_{\epsilon_{i}}(\epsilon_{i}^{-2}\omega),

for some sequence
\epsilon_{i}\to\infty
. Since the cone metric
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
is invariant under rescaling, i.e.
\Phi^{*}_{\epsilon_{i}}(\epsilon_{i}^{-2}\omega_{\mathscr{C}})=\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
, the conditions

\frac{1}{C}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}\leqslant\omega_{\epsilon_{i}}\leqslant C\omega_{\mathscr{C}},\qquad\omega_{\epsilon_{i}}^{m}=\omega_{\mathscr{C}}^{m},

are preserved. The associated a priori estimate of the complex Monge-Ampère equation
\omega_{\epsilon_{i}}^{m}=\omega_{\mathscr{C}}^{m}
implies that there exists a
C^{\infty}_{\text{loc}}(\mathscr{C})
-sublimit
\omega_{\infty}
satisfying (
2.1
) (regularity follows by bootstrapping (
2.4
)). Taking the limit
\epsilon_{i}\to 0
gives an asymptotic limit
\omega_{0}
at
o
, both
\omega_{0}
and
\omega_{\infty}
possibly depending on the sequence. Using heat kernel estimates, this section shows that
\omega_{0}
and
\omega_{\infty}
are (a priori, possibly different) Kähler cone metrics.

To proceed, we introduce the heat kernel
H_{t}
and prove certain regularity statements for
H_{t}
.

Definition 2.12
.

Let
(M,g)
be a Riemannian manifold with Laplacian
\Delta
. A nonnegative continuous function
H_{t}(x,y)\colon\mathbb{R}_{+}\times M\times M\to\mathbb{R}
is a heat kernel of
(M,g)
if the following holds: let
f\in C_{0}^{\infty}(M)
, then

•

The function

(P_{t}f)(x)\coloneqq\int_{M}H_{t}(x,y)f(y)\dvol_{g}(y)

solves the heat equation on
M
:

(\partial_{t}-\Delta_{g})(P_{t}f)(x)=0.

•

As
t\to 0
, then

(P_{t}f)(x)\to f(x)

in
C_{0}^{\infty}(M)
.

Theorem 2.13
.

[
23
, Theorem 7.7, 7.13, and 7.20]
For any Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
, there is a unique and smooth heat kernel
H_{t}(x,y)
such that
H_{t}(\cdot,y)\in L^{2}(M)
for every fixed
t>0,y\in M
, and satisfying:

•

Symmetry:
H_{t}(x,y)=H_{t}(y,x)
.

•

The semigroup identity:

H_{t+s}(x,y)=\int_{M}H_{t}(x,z)H_{s}(z,y)\dvol_{g}(z).

•

For fixed
x\in M
, the function
H_{t}(x,y)
satisfies the heat equation in
(t,y)
:

(2.10)

(\partial_{t}-\Delta_{g})H_{t}(x,y)=0.

•

For any
x\in M
and
t>0
:

\int_{M}H_{t}(x,y)\dvol_{g}(y)\leqslant 1.

In order to prove regularity of the heat kernel of
\omega
, we need to control the curvature tensor.

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
\omega
be a cscK metric on
\mathscr{C}
with a uniform bound
\frac{1}{C}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}\leqslant\omega\leqslant C\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
. For the curvature tensor
\Rm
of
\omega
and for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a constant
C_{m}
such that

(2.11)

\sum_{k=0}^{m}R^{2+k}|\nabla^{k}_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}\Rm|_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(p)\leqslant C_{m},

where
\nabla_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}
is the connection of
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
and
R=\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(o,p)
is the distance to the apex.

Proof.

Pick a point
p\in\mathscr{C}
with
\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(o,p)=R
. Pull back by
\Phi_{R}
such that
\tilde{p}\coloneqq\Phi_{R}^{*}(p)
with
\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(o,\tilde{p})=1
. Assume that
B_{\delta}(\tilde{p})
is trivial for some small enough
\delta>0
, and pick holomorphic normal coordinates
z_{1}=x_{1}+ix_{2},\dots,z_{m}=x_{2m-1}+ix_{2m}
with respect to
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
. Corollary
2.11
implies that
\omega^{m}=\omega_{\mathscr{C}}^{m}
(potentially after rescaling). By
[
11
, Lemma 2.1]
, there exists a potential
\varphi
such that

i\partial\overline{\partial}\varphi=\omega,\quad\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{C^{2,\alpha}(B_{\delta}(p))}\leqslant C,

where
C
depends only on
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
and the uniform bound of
\omega
due to the regularity of the complex Monge-Ampère equation
[
11
, Theorem 1.1]
. Proceeding as in Proposition
2.8
by differentiating
\omega^{m}=\omega_{\mathscr{C}}^{m}
(specifically, as in (
2.4
) but with a different background metric), we obtain the equation:

m\Delta_{\omega}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x_{i}}=\tr_{\omega}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}^{m}\right).

The right-hand side is uniformly bounded in
C^{0,\alpha}(B_{\delta}(\tilde{p}))
. Bootstrapping this equation shows that
\left\lVert\nabla^{k}_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}\varphi\right\rVert_{0,B_{\frac{\delta}{2}}(p)}\leqslant C_{k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, with a constant depending only on
k
and
C
(cf.
[
20
, Theorem 6.17]
for details on the estimates). Covering
\{r=1\}
with finitely many such balls, it follows that
|\nabla^{k}_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}\omega|(\tilde{p})\leqslant C_{k}
for any
\tilde{p}
with
\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(o,\tilde{p})=1
. Expressing
\Rm
as a combination of second derivatives of
\omega
and scaling back shows that
|\nabla^{k}_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}\omega|(p)\leqslant C_{k}R^{-k}
.
∎

Proposition 2.15
.

Let
H_{t}(x,y)
be the heat kernel of
(\mathscr{C},\omega)
. Then, for any fixed
t>0
and
x\in\mathscr{C}
,
\left\lVert H_{t}(x,\cdot)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{C})}\leqslant Ct^{-\frac{1}{m}}
with
C
independent of
x\in\mathscr{C}
. Furthermore,
|\nabla_{\omega}^{k}H_{t}(x,\cdot)|_{\omega}\leqslant C_{k}t^{-\frac{1}{m}}r^{-k}
.

Proof.

First assume that
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathscr{C}\geqslant 3
. For the
L^{\infty}
-bound, fix
\alpha>0
and consider the truncated cone
\mathscr{C}_{\alpha}\coloneqq(\alpha,\alpha^{-1})\times L
with associated heat kernel
H^{\alpha}_{t}
. By
[
21
, Section 8.2.3]
, the heat kernel
H^{\alpha}_{t}
extends to the closure
\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{\alpha}=[\alpha,\alpha^{-1}]\times L
satisfying Dirichlet boundary conditions. Fix
x\in\mathscr{C}_{\alpha}
and set
E_{t}=\int_{\mathscr{C}_{\alpha}}H^{\alpha}_{t}(x,y)^{2}\dvol_{\omega}(y)
for
t>0
and
x\in\mathscr{C}_{\alpha}
. Consider:

\partial_{t}E_{t}=\int_{\mathscr{C}_{\alpha}}2H^{\alpha}_{t}(x,y)\partial_{t}H^{\alpha}_{t}(x,y)\dvol_{\omega}(y)=-2\int_{\mathscr{C}_{\alpha}}|\nabla_{y}H^{\alpha}_{t}(x,y)|^{2}\dvol_{\omega}(y)\leqslant-CE_{t}^{1+\frac{1}{m}},

by the Nash inequality (see Proposition
A.2
). Recall that
m
denotes the complex dimension of
\mathscr{C}_{\alpha}
. ODE comparison implies that
E_{t}\leqslant Ct^{-\frac{1}{m}}
. By the semigroup property of
H^{\alpha}_{t}
:

H_{t}^{\alpha}(x,y)=\int_{\mathscr{C}_{\alpha}}H^{\alpha}_{\frac{t}{2}}(x,z)H^{\alpha}_{\frac{t}{2}}(z,y)\dvol_{\omega}(z)\leqslant\left\lVert H^{\alpha}_{\frac{t}{2}}(x,\cdot)\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{\omega}(\mathscr{C})}\left\lVert H^{\alpha}_{\frac{t}{2}}(\cdot,y)\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{\omega}(\mathscr{C})}\leqslant Ct^{-\frac{1}{m}}.

The constant
C
only depends on the Nash inequality and is independent of
x
and
y
. Thus,
{H_{t}}^{\alpha}\in L^{\infty}_{\omega}(\mathscr{C}_{\alpha}\times\mathscr{C}_{\alpha})
by the uniform bound on
\omega
. As
H^{\alpha}_{t}\to H_{t}
in
C_{\loc}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathscr{C})

[
23
, Exercise 7.40]
and as the Nash constant is uniformly bounded for all
\alpha>0
and
t^{-\frac{1}{m}}
, we obtain
\left\lVert H_{t}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{C})}\leqslant Ct^{\frac{-1}{m}}
for all
t>0
.

To show that
|\nabla^{k}_{\omega}H_{t}(x,\cdot)|_{\omega}\leqslant Ct^{-\frac{1}{m}}r^{-k}
, fix
t>0
and
x\in\mathscr{C}
. We combine Lemma
2.14
, Proposition
B.1
, and the
L^{\infty}
-estimate above as follows: identify
\mathscr{C}\cong\mathbb{R}_{+}\times L
, and let
\chi
be a spacetime bump function on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathscr{C}
with the following properties:
\chi
has support in
[\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2}]\times([\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2}]\times L)
,
\chi\equiv 1
on
[\frac{3}{4},\frac{5}{4}]\times([\frac{3}{4},\frac{5}{4}]\times L)
, and satisfies

\chi^{-\frac{1}{2}}|\nabla\chi|+(\Delta\chi)^{-}+|\partial_{t}\chi|\leqslant C

for some constant
C>0
. Furthermore, define
\beta\colon[\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2}]\times L\to\mathbb{R}
by
\beta(r,x)=r^{2}
with
(r,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times L\cong\mathscr{C}
. For
R=1
, Proposition
B.1
shows that
\left\lVert\nabla^{k}_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}H_{t}(x,\cdot)\right\rVert_{L_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}^{\infty}([\frac{3}{4},\frac{5}{4}])}\leqslant C_{k}
. Pulling back by
\Phi_{R}
, the same argument shows that
|\nabla_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}^{k}H_{t}(x,\cdot)|\leqslant C_{k}R^{-k}
on
[\frac{3R}{4},\frac{5R}{4}]\times L
.
∎

It will be helpful to obtain local upper and lower Gaussian bounds on
H_{t}
. These were proved by Saloff-Coste in
[
44
, Theorem 6.1]
for complete manifolds. The following proposition states and proves a Gaussian upper bound on
\mathscr{C}
by modifying his arguments, which are inspired by Moser’s
[
37
,
38
]
proof of the parabolic Harnack inequality on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Corollary
2.17
extends the result to
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
.

Proposition 2.16
.

For all
t>0
and
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
, there is the following upper bound for the heat kernel of
(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
:

(2.12)

H_{t}(y,y^{\prime})\leqslant\frac{C}{t^{m}}\exp\left(-C_{1}\frac{\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}^{2}(y,y^{\prime})}{t}\right).

The constants
C,C_{1}
only depend on the uniform constant in (
2.1
).

Proof.

The proof in
[
44
]
starts with a complete Riemannian manifold
(M,h)
. On this space, Saloff-Coste studies the heat equation
(\partial_{t}-\tilde{\Delta})u=0
for a Riemannian metric
\tilde{h}
(with Laplacian
\tilde{\Delta}
) uniformly equivalent to
h
. No curvature assumption is placed on
\tilde{h}
, only a lower bound on the Ricci curvature of
h
. In our setup, the manifold
(M,h)
is the cone
(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
, and
\omega
is the uniformly equivalent metric
\tilde{h}
.

The proof in
[
44
]
first shows the existence of Sobolev and weighted Poincaré inequalities
[
44
, Theorem 3.1 + 3.2]
on balls
B_{r}(p)
. The proof of the weighted Poincaré inequality begins with the regular Poincaré inequality on smaller balls
B_{s}(q)\subset B_{r}(p)
. The Poincaré constant is uniformly bounded by
[
6
, p. 214]
. By choosing a Whitney covering of small balls
[
31
, Lemma 5.6]
and applying the Poincaré inequality on each covering ball, the weighted Poincaré inequality is obtained. For further details, see
[
45
, Appendix]
. The suitable Sobolev inequality is proven in
[
44
, Theorem 10.3]
.

All of these arguments go through in our setting if
o\notin\overline{B_{r}(p)}
. We claim that they still hold if
o\in\overline{B_{r}(p)}
. For this, we first need the Sobolev inequality (see Proposition
A.1
for the proof), which is as follows:

\left(\int_{B_{r}(p)}|f|^{2q}\dvol_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}\right)^{1/2q}\leqslant C\int_{B_{r}(p)}|\nabla_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}f|^{2}\dvol_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}},

for
f\in C_{0}^{\infty}(B_{r}(p))
and such that the finite ball
B_{r}(p)
may have closure in
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
containing the apex
o\in\overline{\mathscr{C}}
, and
q=m/(m-1)
(recall that
m
is the complex dimension). This is a stronger version than
[
44
, Theorem 3.1]
, as the integral on the right-hand side only contains
|\nabla f|^{2}
.

For the weighted Poincaré inequality
[
44
, Theorem 3.2]
, we know that the regular Poincaré inequality has a uniformly bounded constant on small balls around the link
\{r=1\}
. As the Poincaré inequality is scale-invariant, we may scale the distance and radius of the balls uniformly to get arbitrarily close to the apex with associated smaller radii. As the Whitney covering in
[
45
]
constructs balls whose radii are proportional to the distance to the boundary, and considering
o\in\overline{\mathscr{C}}
to be part of the boundary for this purpose, this exactly gives the necessary uniform bound on the Poincaré constants of the Whitney covering. The proof, therefore, goes through as in
[
45
]
.

The Harnack inequality
[
44
, Theorem 5.3]
for positive solutions of the heat equation is proved using the methods in Moser
[
38
]
. For a test function
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(B_{r}(p))
, Moser considers integrals of the form:

(2.13)

\int_{B_{r}(p)}\left\langle\nabla_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}\varphi,\nabla_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}u^{p}\right\rangle_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}\dvol_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}},

where
p\geqslant 1
and
u
is a weak solution of the heat equation. For balls such that
o\notin\overline{B_{r}(p)}\subset\overline{\mathscr{C}}
, nothing needs to be changed. If the closure of
B_{r}(p)
in
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
contains the apex, we replace
\varphi
, which may not be in
C^{\infty}_{0}(B_{r}(p))
, with
\chi_{i}\varphi
, where
(\chi_{i})
is a family of smooth bump functions on
\mathscr{C}
with supports away from
B_{\frac{1}{i}}(o)
and such that
\chi_{i}\to 1
pointwise on
B_{r}(p)
as
i\to\infty
. By basic scaling, we can fix
\chi_{i}
such that
|\nabla\chi_{i}|\leqslant\frac{C}{i}
on
B_{\frac{2}{i}}(o)
for some
C>0
and
\nabla\chi_{i}=0
on
\mathscr{C}\setminus B_{\frac{2}{i}}(o)
. Then:

\displaystyle\int_{B_{r}(p)}\left\langle\nabla_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(\chi_{i}\varphi),\nabla_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}u^{p}\right\rangle_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}\dvol_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{B_{r}(p)}pu^{p-1}\chi_{i}\left\langle\nabla_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}\varphi,\nabla_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}u\right\rangle\dvol_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}+\int_{B_{r}(p)}pu^{p-1}\varphi\left\langle\nabla_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}\chi_{i},\nabla_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}u\right\rangle\dvol_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}.

Assume that
u\in L^{\infty}_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(B_{r}(p))
and that both
u
and its weak gradient
\nabla_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}u
have finite
L^{2}(B_{r}(p))
-norm. Use Cauchy-Schwarz, dominated convergence, and the bound above to show that the second integral on the right-hand side vanishes in the limit
i\to\infty
. Therefore, all arguments in Moser can be carried out for bump functions with support away from the apex. Afterwards, we take the limit
i\to\infty
to include the apex.

Let
Q=[0,\tau]\times B_{R}(p)
for some
p\in\overline{\mathscr{C}}
, and define
Q_{-}=(\tau_{1}^{-},\tau_{2}^{-})\times B_{R^{\prime}}(p^{\prime}),Q_{+}=(\tau^{+},\tau)\times B_{R^{\prime}}(p^{\prime})
for
R^{\prime}<R
and
0<\tau_{1}^{-}<\tau_{2}^{-}<\tau^{+}<\tau
. The obtained Harnack inequality (see
[
37
, Theorem 1]
and
[
44
, Theorem 5.3]
) now reads:

(2.14)

\sup_{Q_{-}}u\leqslant C\inf_{Q_{+}}u,

for any nonnegative solution
u
of the heat equation on
Q
, where
C=C(R,R^{\prime},\tau_{1}^{-},\tau_{2}^{-},\tau^{+},\tau)
and the constant of the uniform bound in (
2.1
). If
u
is positive, a chaining argument (see
[
37
, Proof of Theorem 2]
) shows that:

(2.15)

\log\left(\frac{u(t^{\prime},y^{\prime})}{u(t,y)}\right)\leqslant C\left(\frac{\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(y,y^{\prime})^{2}}{t-t^{\prime}}+1\right),

where
C
only depends on the constant of the uniform bound in (
2.1
). Furthermore, for any positive solution
u
(see
[
44
, Corollary 5.3]
):

(2.16)

|u(t^{\prime},y^{\prime})-u(t,y)|\leqslant C\left(\frac{\max\{\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(y,y^{\prime}),\sqrt{|t-t^{\prime}|}\}}{r}\right)^{\gamma}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}([s-r^{2},s]\times B_{r}(p))},

for
t,t^{\prime}\in[s-\delta r^{2},s]
,
y,y^{\prime}\in B_{\delta r}(p)
, any
s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and where
C,\gamma
only depend on the constant in (
2.1
) and
0<\delta<1
.

To finally obtain the Gaussian upper bounds in
[
44
, Theorem 6.1 and 6.3]
, the proof in
[
44
, Theorem 6.3]
only necessitates that the heat kernel generates the heat flow. Saloff-Coste combines this with simple results in complex analysis and the Harnack inequality to show the upper bound. Our bounds simplify compared to the work of Saloff-Coste as
\Ric(\omega_{\mathscr{C}})=0
.
∎

Corollary 2.17
.

The heat kernel
H_{t}(x,y)
on
\mathscr{C}
has a unique continuous extension to
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
satisfying the Gaussian upper bounds:

(2.17)

|\nabla_{\omega,y}^{k}H_{t}(y,y^{\prime})|\leqslant\frac{C_{k}}{t^{m}}\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(o,y)^{-k}\exp\left(-C_{1}\frac{\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}^{2}(y,y^{\prime})}{t}\right),

for all
y^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathscr{C}}
and
y\in\mathscr{C}
. Furthermore,
\left\lVert H_{t}(x,\cdot)\right\rVert_{L_{\omega}^{1}(\mathscr{C})}=1
for all
x\in\overline{\mathscr{C}}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.15
, we know that
\left\lVert H_{t}(x,\cdot)\right\rVert_{L_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}^{\infty}(\mathscr{C})}\leqslant C
, with
C
independent of
x
. Using the Hölder continuity property (
2.16
),
H_{t}(x,y)
has a unique continuous extension to
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
, and
H_{t}(x,\cdot)\to H_{t}(o,\cdot)
as
x\to o
in
C^{0,\gamma}_{\loc}(\overline{\mathscr{C}})
by (
2.16
). By parabolic regularity,
H_{t}(o,\cdot)
satisfies the heat equation (
2.10
) and
H_{t}(x,\cdot)\to H_{t}(o,\cdot)
in
C^{\infty}_{\loc}(\mathscr{C})
. As
H_{t}(x,\cdot)
satisfies the Gaussian upper bounds of Theorem
2.12
uniformly, so does the extension
H_{t}(o,\cdot)
. Finally, dominated convergence shows that
\left\lVert H_{t}(o,\cdot)\right\rVert_{L^{1}_{\omega}(\mathscr{C})}\leqslant 1
. Hence, from now on, we assume that
H_{t}
is a function
H_{t}\colon\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\overline{\mathscr{C}}\times\overline{\mathscr{C}}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

The estimates on the derivatives follows by the same argument as in Proposition
2.15
.

Finally, the equality
\left\lVert H_{t}(x,\cdot)\right\rVert_{L^{1}_{\omega}(\mathscr{C})}=1
for all
x\in\overline{\mathscr{C}}
is a direct consequence of the nonnegativity of
H_{t}
and the Gaussian bounds in (
2.17
):

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\left\lVert H_{t}(x,\cdot)\right\rVert_{L^{1}_{\omega}(\mathscr{C})}

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\int_{\mathscr{C}}H_{t}(x,y)\dvol_{\omega}(y)=\int_{\mathscr{C}}\partial_{t}H_{t}(x,y)\dvol_{\omega}(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathscr{C}}\Delta_{\omega}H_{t}(x,y)\dvol_{\omega}(y)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\alpha\to 0}\int_{\partial([\alpha,\alpha^{-1}]\times L)}\left\langle\nabla_{\omega}H_{t}(x,y),\nu_{\omega}\right\rangle\dvol_{\omega}(y)=0,

so
\left\lVert H_{t}(x,\cdot)\right\rVert_{L^{1}_{\omega}(\mathscr{C})}
is invariant in time. Finally, the property
(P_{t}f)\to f
in
C^{\infty}_{\loc}(\mathscr{C})
for all bump functions
f\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathscr{C})
as
t\to 0^{+}
shows that
\left\lVert P_{t}f\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}(\mathscr{C})}\to\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}(\mathscr{C})}
. Young’s inequality
\left\lVert P_{t}f\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}(\mathscr{C})}\leqslant\left\lVert H_{t}(x,\cdot)\right\rVert_{L^{1}_{\omega}(\mathscr{C})}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}(\mathscr{C})}
hence concludes that
\left\lVert H_{t}(x,\cdot)\right\rVert_{L^{1}_{\omega}(\mathscr{C})}\to 1
as
t\to 0^{+}
. For
x=o
, take a sequence
x_{i}\to o
and use dominated convergence and the Gaussian upper bounds of (
2.17
) to conclude that the
L_{\omega}^{1}
-norm does not escape to infinity, thus
\left\lVert H_{t}(o,\cdot)\right\rVert_{L^{1}_{\omega}(\mathscr{C})}=1
.
∎

Corollary 2.18
.

The heat kernel
H_{t}
satisfies the Gaussian lower bound:

H_{t}(y,y^{\prime})\geqslant\frac{C}{t^{m}}\exp\left(-C_{1}\frac{\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}^{2}(y,y^{\prime})}{t}\right),

for all
y,y^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathscr{C}}
.

Proof.

By picking a ball
B_{R}(o)
large enough, the Harnack inequality (
2.14
) and
\left\lVert H_{t}(p,\cdot)\right\rVert_{L_{\omega}^{1}(\mathscr{C})}=1
for all
p\in\mathscr{C}
show that
H_{t}(p,\cdot)>0
everywhere and for any
p\in\overline{\mathscr{C}}
. Pick
y,y^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathscr{C}}
,
0<t^{\prime}=\frac{t}{2}
, and set
u(t,\cdot)=H_{t}(y^{\prime},\cdot)
The log-inequality (
2.15
) states that

(2.18)

u(t,y)\geqslant C\exp\left(-C_{1}\frac{\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(y,y^{\prime})^{2}}{t-t^{\prime}}\right)u(t^{\prime},y^{\prime}).

Since (
2.18
) is invariant under a parabolic rescaling, rescale
(t,x)\mapsto(\tilde{t},\tilde{x})=(\frac{t}{t^{\prime}},\frac{x}{\sqrt{t^{\prime}}})
such that
t^{\prime}\mapsto 1
, and let
\tilde{u}(\tilde{t},\tilde{x})=u(t,x)
. Under this rescaling, the Harnack inequality (
2.14
) further shows that

\displaystyle\tilde{u}(2,\tilde{y})

\displaystyle\geqslant C\exp\left(-2C_{1}\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(\tilde{y},\tilde{y}^{\prime})^{2}\right)\tilde{u}(1,\tilde{y}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\geqslant C\exp\left(-2C_{1}\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(\tilde{y},\tilde{y}^{\prime})^{2}\right)\sup_{x\in B_{R}(\tilde{y}^{\prime})}\tilde{u}\left(\frac{1}{2},x\right).

The constant in the Harnack inequality only depends on
R>0
. Pick
R
such that

\int_{B_{R}(\tilde{y}^{\prime})}\tilde{u}\left(\frac{1}{2},x\right)\,\dvol_{\tilde{\omega}}(x)\geqslant\frac{1}{2(t^{\prime})^{m}}=\frac{2^{m-1}}{t^{m}}.

Here,
\tilde{\omega}=(t^{\prime})^{-1}\Phi^{*}_{\sqrt{t^{\prime}}}\omega
is uniformly equivalent to
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
. Such an
R
exists and is independent of
\tilde{y}^{\prime}
by the Gaussian estimates of Corollary
2.17
and
\left\lVert H_{\frac{t^{\prime}}{2}}(p,\cdot)\right\rVert_{L_{\omega}^{1}(\mathscr{C})}=1
. The factor
\frac{1}{(t^{\prime})^{m}}
arises from the parabolic rescaling. Hence:

\displaystyle\tilde{u}(2,\tilde{y})

\displaystyle\geqslant C\exp\left(-2C_{1}\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(\tilde{y},\tilde{y}^{\prime})^{2}\right)\sup_{x\in B_{R}(\tilde{y}^{\prime})}\tilde{u}\left(\frac{1}{2},x\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant C\exp\left(-2C_{1}\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(\tilde{y},\tilde{y}^{\prime})^{2}\right)\frac{1}{\vol_{\tilde{\omega}}(B_{R}(\tilde{y}^{\prime}))}\int_{B_{R}(\tilde{y}^{\prime})}\tilde{u}\left(\frac{1}{2},x\right)\,\dvol_{\tilde{\omega}}(x)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{C}{t^{m}}\exp\left(-2C_{1}\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(\tilde{y},\tilde{y}^{\prime})^{2}\right),

where
C
absorbs the factor
\vol_{\tilde{\omega}}(B_{R}(\tilde{y}^{\prime}))^{-1}
, which is uniformly bounded. Scale back and the result follows.
∎

If
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathscr{C}=2
, the heat kernel extends smoothly over the apex.

Corollary 2.19
.

H_{t}
extends smoothly over the apex when
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathscr{C}=2
.

Proof.

If
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathscr{C}=2
, then
\overline{\mathscr{C}}\cong\mathbb{C}^{2}/\Gamma
for a finite group
\Gamma\subset\SU(m)
. As
\mathscr{C}
is simply-connected, then
\Gamma=\{\Id\}
. If
\omega^{m}=e^{F}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}^{m}
, then
\Delta_{\omega_{\eucl}}F=0
as
\omega
is Ricci-flat. Therefore, the Harnack inequality for harmonic functions on
\mathbb{C}^{m}
, Proposition
2.8
, and bootstrapping imply that
\omega
extends smoothly over
0
. Parabolic regularity
[
33
, Theorem 2.12]
shows that
H_{t}
extends to a smooth function on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
.
∎

Corollary 2.20
.

Let
H_{\epsilon_{i},t}
be the heat kernel of
\omega_{\epsilon_{i}}
, and denote by
H_{\infty,t}
a
C^{\infty}_{\loc}(\mathscr{C})
sublimit of
H_{\epsilon_{i},t}
as
\epsilon_{i}\to\infty
. Then
H_{\infty,t}
satisfies:

•

Gaussian upper and lower bounds of Corollaries
2.17
and
2.18
.

•

Smooth extension over
o
if
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathscr{C}=2
.

•

\left\lVert H_{\infty,t}(x,\cdot)\right\rVert_{L^{1}_{\omega_{\infty}}(\mathscr{C})}=1
.

Proof.

As
H_{\infty,t}
is the
C^{\infty}_{\loc}(\mathscr{C})
-limit of
H_{\epsilon_{i},t}
, the Gaussian upper and lower bounds in Proposition
2.12
hold uniformly and so pass along the sequence. The extension to
o
follows by the Hölder estimate (
2.16
). Then
\left\lVert H_{\infty,t}\right\rVert_{L^{1}_{\omega_{\infty}}(\mathscr{C})}=1
as
\left\lVert H_{\epsilon_{i},t}\right\rVert_{L^{1}_{\omega_{\epsilon_{i}}}(\mathscr{C})}=1
by dominated convergence and the Gaussian upper bounds.
∎

Theorem 2.21
.

Define
f_{\infty,t}\colon\overline{\mathscr{C}}\times\overline{\mathscr{C}}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
via

(2.19)

H_{\infty,t}(x,y)=(4\pi t)^{-m}e^{-f_{\infty,t}(x,y)}.

Then, the asymptotic limit
\omega_{\infty}
is a cone metric with link given by the level set
\{f_{\infty,t}(o,\cdot)=c\}
for any fixed
t>0
and some constant
c
depending on
t
.

Proof.

Define
f_{t}
for the heat kernel of
\omega
as in (
2.19
) and

u(t,y)\coloneqq H_{t}(o,y)

as the heat kernel of
\omega
with base point
o
. Following Perelman
[
41
]
, define the entropy-like quantity:

(2.20)

\mathcal{W}(t)\coloneqq\int_{\mathscr{C}}\left(t|\nabla_{\omega}f_{t}(o,y)|_{\omega}^{2}+f_{t}(o,y)-2m\right)u(t,y)\dvol_{\omega}(y).

\mathcal{W}(t)
exists due to the Gaussian upper and lower bounds of Corollaries
2.17
and
2.18
for
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathscr{C}\geqslant 3
. If
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}=2
,
H_{t}
extends smoothly over
o
and the integral also exists due to the Gaussian upper bounds at infinity.

Using the Bochner formula and
\left\lVert u(t,\cdot)\right\rVert_{L^{1}_{\omega}(\mathscr{C})}=1
,
[
16
, proof of Theorem 16.8]
shows that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{W}(t)=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathscr{C}}(u(t,\cdot)\Delta_{\omega}W(t)+2\left\langle\nabla_{\omega}u(t,\cdot),\nabla_{\omega}W(t)\right\rangle_{\omega}+W\Delta_{\omega}u(t,\cdot))\dvol_{\omega}

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathscr{C}}2t\left|\nabla_{\omega}^{2}f_{t}(o,\cdot)-\frac{1}{2t}g\right|^{2}_{\omega}\dvol_{\omega},

where
\Ric(g)=0
,
g
is the metric tensor of
\omega
, and
W(t)=t(\Delta_{\omega}f_{t}(o,y)-|\nabla_{\omega}f_{t}(o,y)|_{\omega}^{2})+f_{t}(o,y)-2m
. The proof uses nothing but
u(t,\cdot)
being a positive solution of the heat equation and
\left\lVert u(t,\cdot)\right\rVert_{L^{1}_{\omega}(\mathscr{C})}=1
. Restricting the integral to
\mathscr{C}_{\alpha}=[\alpha,\alpha^{-1}]\times L
and using integration by parts cancels terms inside the integral, leaving boundary terms. These boundary terms scale as
R^{-3}
at the apex if
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\geqslant 3
, so combining this with the Gaussian bounds (
2.12
) for the boundary at infinity shows that the integral of the boundary terms vanishes as
\alpha\to 0
. For
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathscr{C}=2
, the heat kernel extends smoothly over
o
and the boundary integral therefore also vanishes. Thus:

(2.21)

\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{W}(t)=-\int_{\mathscr{C}}2t\left|\nabla_{\omega}^{2}f_{t}(o,\cdot)-\frac{1}{2t}g\right|^{2}_{\omega}\dvol_{\omega}.

Repeat the above procedure for the rescaled metric
\omega_{\epsilon}
with associated heat kernel
H_{\epsilon,t}(x,y)=\Phi_{\epsilon_{i}}^{*}H_{\epsilon_{i}^{2}t}(x,y)
, where
\Phi_{\epsilon_{i}}(x,y)=(\epsilon_{i}x,\epsilon_{i}y)
. Since
x=o
is fixed,
\mathcal{W}(t)
is invariant under this rescaling in the following sense: let
\mathcal{W}_{\epsilon}(t)
be the quantity (
2.20
) for
\omega_{\epsilon}
, then

(2.22)

\mathcal{W}_{\epsilon}(t)=\mathcal{W}(\epsilon^{2}t).

The integrals
\mathcal{W}_{\epsilon}(t)
are uniformly bounded independent of
t>0
due to the uniform Gaussian bounds on all
H_{\epsilon_{i},t}
. Letting
\epsilon_{i}\to\infty
, the sequence
\omega_{\epsilon_{i}}\to\omega_{\infty}
converges in
C_{\loc}^{\infty}(\mathscr{C})
, and the heat kernels
H_{\epsilon_{i},t}\to H_{\infty,t}
converge in
C_{\loc}^{\infty}(\mathscr{C})
. Since the integrand of
\mathcal{W}_{\epsilon}(t)
is uniformly bounded by the Gaussian bounds, the limit
\mathcal{W}_{\epsilon}(t)\to\mathcal{W}_{\infty}(t)
exists in
C^{0}_{\loc}((0,\infty))
by dominated convergence. The monotonicity (
2.22
) implies that
\mathcal{W}_{\infty}(t)
is constant in
t
. Hence
\partial_{t}\mathcal{W}_{\infty}(t)=0
, and (
2.21
) shows that
\nabla_{\omega_{\infty}}^{2}f_{\infty,t}(o,\cdot)=\frac{\omega_{\infty}}{2t}
, i.e.,

(2.23)

\mathscr{L}_{\nabla_{\omega_{\infty}}f_{\infty,t}(o,\cdot)}\omega_{\infty}=\frac{\omega_{\infty}}{4t},

where
\nabla_{\omega_{\infty}}
is the connection with respect to
\omega_{\infty}
.

We now show that
\omega_{\infty}
is isometric to a conical metric. For the proof on complete manifolds, see
[
48
, Theorem 1]
. To show that
\omega_{\infty}
is a conical metric, first assume
\nabla_{\omega_{\infty}}f_{\infty,t}(o,\cdot)\neq 0
everywhere on
\mathscr{C}
. Then all level sets are smooth, and the gradient flow of
f_{\infty,t}(o,\cdot)
defines a local isometry near any level set
\{f_{\infty,t}(o,\cdot)=c\}
. Since
\mathscr{L}_{\nabla_{\omega_{\infty}}f_{\infty,t}(o,\cdot)}f_{\infty,t}(o,\cdot)=|\nabla_{\omega_{\infty}}f_{\infty,t}(o,\cdot)|^{2}>0
,
f_{\infty,t}(o,\cdot)
is strictly increasing along its flow. If the flow stopped,
\mathscr{L}_{\nabla_{\omega_{\infty}}f_{\infty,t}(o,\cdot)}f_{\infty,t}(o,\cdot)=0
, which leads to a contradiction. Thus,
\omega_{\infty}
is a conical metric.

If
\nabla_{\omega_{\infty}}f_{t}(o,z)=0
at some point
z\in\mathscr{C}
, then (
2.23
) and the nondegeneracy of
\omega_{\infty}
show that
z
is an isolated critical point. Assume that
B_{\epsilon}(z)
has no other critical points. By the Morse lemma, the level sets of
f_{\infty}
are diffeomorphic to
S^{2m-1}
, and the previous argument shows that
\omega_{\infty}|_{B_{\epsilon}(z)}
is isometric to a cone metric. As the metric extends smoothly over
z
,
\omega_{\infty}|_{B_{\epsilon}(z)}
is flat. As
\omega_{\infty}
is Ricci-flat,
[
18
, Theorem 5.2]
shows that
|\Rm_{\omega}|^{2}
is analytic, and as the curvature vanishes on
B_{\epsilon}(z)
,
|\Rm_{\omega}|^{2}
vanishes everywhere. We conclude that
\omega_{\infty}
is a cone metric on
\mathscr{C}
.
∎

Corollary 2.22
.

By letting
\epsilon_{i}\to 0
, the same proof as in Theorem
2.21
shows that
\omega_{0}
is isometric to a conical metric.

By Theorem
2.21
and Corollary
2.22
,
\omega_{\infty}
and
\omega_{0}
are both isometric to, a priori different, conical metrics on
\mathscr{C}
.

2.3.
The Tangent Cones and
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
have equal Reeb fields

This section shows that the tangent cones
\omega_{0}
,
\omega_{\infty}
, and the original cone metric
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
have equal Reeb fields (resp. scaling vector fields). All of the arguments in this section are done for
\omega_{\infty}
, but work equally well for
\omega_{0}
.

2.3.1.
Commuting Reeb Fields

Let
V
be the scaling vector field of
\omega_{\infty}
, and
r\partial_{r}
the scaling vector field of
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
. The first step is to show that
[V,r\partial_{r}]=0
. The proof follows arguments outlined in
[
28
]
, wherein a classification of holomorphic vector fields commuting with
r\partial_{r}
is presented. Employing the fact that
V
is holomorphic and has linear growth due to the uniform bound (
2.1
), i.e.
\frac{r}{C}\leqslant|V|_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}\leqslant Cr
for some
C>0
, we modify the proofs to accommodate our situation and thereby show that
V
is homogeneous and is given by this classification. Define

\psi(\cdot)\coloneqq g_{\mathscr{C}}(V,\cdot)=\omega_{\mathscr{C}}(JV,\cdot)\in\Omega^{1}(\mathscr{C})

to be the dual of
V
with respect to the cone metric
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
.

Lemma 2.23
.

V
is holomorphic for the complex structure
J
of
\mathscr{C}
, and the one-form
\psi
dual to
V
is harmonic with respect to
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
.

Proof.

Since
\omega_{\epsilon_{i}}
are all Kähler with respect to
J
and
\omega_{\infty}
is the
C^{\infty}_{\loc}
–limit of
(\omega_{\epsilon_{i}})
, then
\omega_{\infty}
is Kähler for
J
and
V
is holomorphic for the complex structure. Recall the Bochner formulas for vector fields
X\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathscr{C})
and one-forms
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(\mathscr{C})
, cf.
[
2
, eq. (4.80)]
:

\displaystyle 2\overline{\partial}^{*}\overline{\partial}X

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}^{*}\nabla_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}X-\Ric_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(X),

\displaystyle(dd^{*}+d^{*}d)\alpha

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}^{*}\nabla_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}\alpha+\Ric_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(\alpha).

Since
\overline{\partial}V=0
and
\Ric_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}=0
, we have
\nabla_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}^{*}\nabla_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}V=0
. Finally,
\nabla_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}^{*}\nabla_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}\psi=0
as
\psi
is the dual of
V
, so the second equation implies that
(dd^{*}+d^{*}d)\psi=0
and thus
\psi
is harmonic with respect to
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
.
∎

To use the classification by Hein-Sun
[
28
, Theorem 2.14]
for
V
, it is necessary to show that the following lemma applies to
\psi
. Lemma
2.24
was originally formulated by Cheeger-Tian in
[
9
]
, but we use the following formulation:

Lemma 2.24

(
[
28
, Lemma B.1]
)
.

Let
C(Y)
be a Riemannian cone of dimension
n\geqslant 3
such that
\Ric C(Y)\geqslant 0
. Let
\alpha
be a homogeneous 1-form on
C(Y)
with growth rate in
[0,1]
. Then
(dd^{*}+d^{*}d)\alpha=0
holds if and only if, up to linear combination, either
\alpha=d(r^{\mu}\kappa)
, where
\kappa
is a
\lambda
-eigenfunction on Y for some
\lambda\in[n-1,2n]
and
\mu
is chosen so that
r^{\mu}\varphi
is a harmonic function on
C(Y)
, or
\alpha=r^{2}\eta
, where
\mathscr{L}_{r\partial_{r}}\eta=0
and, at
\{r=1\}
,
\eta^{\sharp}
is a Killing field on Y with
\Ric_{Y}\eta^{\sharp}=(n-2)\eta^{\sharp}
, or
\alpha=rdr
.

As
V
is the scaling vector field of
\omega_{\infty}
, it has linear growth with respect to this metric. Furthermore, the uniform boundedness in (
2.1
) implies that
|V|_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}
is comparable to
r
, and hence this also holds for the dual
\psi
. Thus, to apply Lemma
2.24
to
\psi
, it is necessary to prove the following:

Lemma 2.25
.

The conclusion of Lemma
2.24
holds if "homogeneous 1-form
\alpha
with growth rate in
[0,1]
" is replaced by "1-form
\alpha
with upper and lower bound
\frac{r}{C}\leqslant|\alpha|_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}\leqslant Cr
".

Proof.

The proof
[
28
, Lemma B.1]
proceeds by decomposing the 1-form
\alpha
into a sum of eigenfunctions
f(x)r^{p}\log^{q}r
, where
f
is a one-form depending on the link (but could contain a
dr
-term),
p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
q\in\{0,1\}
. After solving
(dd^{*}+d^{*}d)f(x)r^{p}\log^{q}r=0
for each term separately, the uniform bound
\frac{r}{c}\leqslant|\alpha|_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}\leqslant Cr
implies that all terms except the linear growth term with no
\log
-factor vanish and the remaining term is homogeneous by construction. The argument is very similar to the proof of Lemma
C.1
. The rest of the proof follows as for Lemma
2.24
.
∎

Now, the following theorem severely restricts the form of the scaling vector field
V
of
\omega_{\infty}
.

Theorem 2.26
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
be a Calabi-Yau cone and
\mathfrak{p}
be the span of
r\partial_{r}
and the gradient fields of all
\xi
-invariant 2-homogeneous harmonic functions (see Lemma
2.24
) of
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
. Then the space of all holomorphic vector fields commuting with
r\partial_{r}
is equal to
\mathfrak{p}\oplus J\mathfrak{p}
. Furthermore, the scaling vector field
V
of
\omega_{\infty}
satisfies
[r\partial_{r},V]=0
. Therefore,
V\in\mathfrak{p}\oplus J\mathfrak{p}
with all elements in
J\mathfrak{p}
Killing fields of
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
.

Proof.

[
28
, Theorem 2.14]
shows that the space of all holomorphic vector fields commuting with
r\partial_{r}
is equal to
\mathfrak{p}\oplus J\mathfrak{p}
. Next, as
V
is homogeneous in
r
by Lemma
2.25
, then

[r\partial_{r},V]=\mathscr{L}_{r\partial_{r}}V=\mu V.

For any
(p,q)
-tensor
T
,
\mathscr{L}_{r\partial_{r}}T=\mu T
implies that
|T|=\mathcal{O}(r^{\mu+p-q})
.
V
is a
(1,0)
-tensor with linear growth, i.e.
\mu+1=1
, thus
\mu=0
and
[r\partial_{r},V]=0
, and we conclude that
V\in\mathfrak{p}\oplus J\mathfrak{p}
.
∎

2.3.2.
Reeb fields are Killing fields

The next step is to prove that
\xi
, the Reeb field of
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
, acts by isometries on the tangent cone
\omega_{\infty}
, and vice versa for the Reeb field
JV
of
\omega_{\infty}
on
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
. Since
JV
is holomorphic and commutes with
r\partial_{r}
, it is enough to show that
JV
has no
\mathfrak{p}
-component in the decomposition
\mathfrak{p}\oplus J\mathfrak{p}
from Theorem
2.26
(recall that
J\mathfrak{p}
consists of Killing fields for
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
). This is accomplished by embedding the cone into
\mathbb{C}^{N}
and applying the Jordan-Chevalley decomposition to
JV
.

Ornea-Verbitsky first proved the following lemma in
[
40
, Theorem 1.2]
. However, we use the formulation of van Coevering
[
49
, Theorem 3.1]
and slightly strengthen it to include an embedding of the holomorphic automorphism
\Aut_{\Scl}(\mathscr{C})
, which commutes with scaling.

Lemma 2.27
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
be a Calabi-Yau cone with a torus
\mathbb{T}^{k}
acting by holomorphic isometries. Assume furthermore that the Reeb vector field
\xi\in\Lie(\mathbb{T}^{k})
lie in the Lie algebra of
\mathbb{T}^{k}
and all elements in
\mathbb{T}^{k}
commute with
\Aut_{\Scl}(\mathscr{C})
. Then, for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a holomorphic embedding
\Phi\colon\mathscr{C}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{N}
equivariant with respect to some homomorphism
\varphi\colon\Aut_{\Scl}(\mathscr{C})\hookrightarrow\GL(N,\mathbb{C})
. Furthermore, there is a choice of hermitian inner product on
\mathbb{C}^{N}
such that
\Isom(\omega_{\mathscr{C}})\cap\Aut_{\Scl}(\mathscr{C})\hookrightarrow\U(N)
and
\mathbb{T}^{k}\hookrightarrow\U(1)^{N}
.

Proof.

The proof in
[
49
, Theorem 3.1]
provides a weight-space decomposition of functions on
\mathscr{C}
as follows: let
\mathbb{T}^{k}
be the given torus acting by holomorphic isometries on
(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
. Let
f
be a holomorphic function on some open set
U\subset\mathscr{C}
with closure in
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
containing
o
and invariant under the action of
\mathbb{T}^{k}
. Let
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
and
\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})\in\mathbb{T}^{k}
. Define

(2.24)

f_{\mathbf{a}}(z)\coloneqq\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{k}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{k}}\prod_{j=1}^{k}t_{j}^{-1-a_{j}}f(t\cdot z)dt.

By a change of variable, one easily sees that

f_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbf{t}\cdot z)=\mathbf{t}^{\mathbf{a}}f_{\mathbf{a}}(z)\coloneqq t_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdots t_{k}^{a_{k}}f_{\mathbf{a}}(z).

Then there is the decomposition

f(z)=\sum_{(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}f_{(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})}(z),

with the right-hand side converging in
C_{\loc}^{\infty}(\mathscr{C})
. We call each
f_{\mathbf{a}}
an
\mathbf{a}
-eigenfunction of
\mathbb{T}^{k}
. The proof of
[
49
, Theorem 3.1]
shows that for a finite set of functions
f_{1},\dots,f_{l}
defining an embedding around a small neighborhood of
o\in\overline{\mathscr{C}}
, we can pick finitely many components
(f_{j})_{\mathbf{a}_{j}}
,
j=1,\dots,d
, and for different
\mathbf{a}_{j}=(a_{1,j},\dots,a_{k,j})
. All functions in
(f_{j})_{\mathbf{a}_{j}}
are holomorphic, and as
r\partial_{r}
is in the complexification
\Lie(\mathbb{T}^{k})\otimes\mathbb{C}
, all
(f_{j})_{\mathbf{a}_{j}}
are homogeneous and hence determine an embedding by

\tilde{\Phi}\colon\mathscr{C}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{d},\quad p\mapsto\left((f_{1})_{\mathbf{a}_{1}}(p),\dots,(f_{d})_{\mathbf{a}_{d}}(p)\right).

Next, we extend the embedding to some larger
\mathbb{C}^{N}
for
N\geqslant d
to define the homomorphism
\varphi\colon\Aut_{\Scl}(\mathscr{C})\to\GL(N,\mathbb{C})
as follows: let
V
be the span of all functions
f\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathbb{C}
such that
f=f_{\mathbf{a}}
is an
\mathbf{a}
-eigenfunction of
\mathbb{T}^{k}
for a sequence
\mathbf{a}
coming from one of the embedding coordinates of
\tilde{\Phi}
. There are only finitely many different embedding functions
f_{\mathbf{a}}
, and so finitely many
\mathbf{a}
for
\tilde{\Phi}
. Each function
f_{\mathbf{a}}
is
d
-homogeneous for some
d
depending on
\mathbf{a}
, and as
f_{\mathbf{a}}
is furthermore holomorphic and hence harmonic, then

\Delta_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}f_{\mathbf{a}}=\frac{1}{r^{2}}(d(d-1)+d(2m-1)+\Delta_{L})f_{\mathbf{a}}=0.

Thus,
f_{\mathbf{a}}|_{L}
is an eigenfunction of
\Delta_{L}
with eigenvalue
-d(d-1)-d(2m-1)
. As the space of such eigenfunctions is finite-dimensional for each
d
,
V
is finite-dimensional.

Next, if
f_{\mathbf{a}}\in V
is an
\mathbf{a}
-eigenfunction,
\Psi^{*}f_{\mathbf{a}}
for
\Psi\in\Aut_{\Scl}(\mathscr{C})
is also an
\mathbf{a}
-eigenfunction as
\Aut_{\Scl}(\mathscr{C})
commutes with
\mathbb{T}^{k}
. Thus,
\Aut_{\Scl}(\mathscr{C})
defines a natural linear action on
V
by pullback. Define an embedding into the dual space by evaluation:

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow V^{*}

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle\mapsto(V\ni f\mapsto f(p)).

The action of
\Aut_{\Scl}(\mathscr{C})
on
V
extends to the dual space
V^{*}
, preserves
\mathscr{C}\subset V^{*}
, and is compatible with the action on
\mathscr{C}
. More specifically, let
\varphi\colon\Aut_{\Scl}(\mathscr{C})\to\GL(V^{*})
denote the induced homomorphism; then, the action on
p\in V^{*}
is

(\varphi(\Psi)p)(f)=p(\Psi^{*}f),

and if
p\in\mathscr{C}\subset V^{*}
, then

(\varphi(\Psi)p)(f)=p(\Psi^{*}f)=f(\Psi(p)).

Consider
V^{*}=\mathbb{C}^{N}
for some
N
to obtain the embedding
\Phi\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathbb{C}^{N}
and homomorphism
\varphi\colon\Aut_{\Scl}(\mathscr{C})\to\GL(N,\mathbb{C})
.
\varphi
is injective as
\Phi
is injective.

Finally,
\Isom(\omega_{\mathscr{C}})\cap\Aut_{\Scl}(\mathscr{C})
is a compact subgroup of
\GL(N,\mathscr{C})
, so averaging the standard Euclidean metric
g_{\text{Eucl}}
on
\mathbb{C}^{N}
over this group makes it invariant under
\Isom(\omega_{\mathscr{C}})\cap\Aut_{\Scl}(\mathscr{C})
, hence
\Isom(\omega_{\mathscr{C}})\cap\Aut_{\Scl}(\mathscr{C})\to\U(N)
for this choice of metric. As the coordinates
(f_{j})_{\mathbf{a}_{j}}
are eigenvectors of the
\mathbb{T}^{k}
-action, then
\mathbb{T}^{k}\hookrightarrow\U(1)^{N}
.
∎

Proposition 2.28
.

The Reeb vector field
JV
of
\omega_{\infty}
is a Killing field for
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
, and the Reeb vector field
\xi
of
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
is a Killing field for
\omega_{\infty}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.27
, there is an embedding
\mathscr{C}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{N}
such that
J\mathfrak{p}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{u}(N)
as
J\mathfrak{p}
consists of holomorphic Killing fields whose flows preserve the scaling vector field
r\partial_{r}
and fix
o
. Because
JV\in\mathfrak{p}\oplus J\mathfrak{p}
, the pushforward of
JV
to
\mathbb{C}^{N}
lies in
\mathfrak{gl}(N,\mathbb{C})
. Thus, we regard
J\mathfrak{p}\subset\mathfrak{u}(N)
,
\mathfrak{p}\subset i\mathfrak{u}(N)
, and
JV\in\mathfrak{gl}(N,\mathbb{C})
. By the Jordan-Chevalley decomposition of
\mathfrak{gl}(N,\mathbb{C})
, we find
D
and
K
such that
D
is semisimple,
K
is nilpotent,
[D,K]=0
, and
JV=D+K
. As
JV
has compact orbits, the same is true for
D
and
K
separately as
D
has exponential flow and
K
has polynomial flow. This means the flows cannot combine to form compact orbits if both flows do not already have compact orbits. As
D
is diagonalizable, all eigenvalues are strictly imaginary; otherwise, write

ADA^{-1}=\diag(a_{1},\dots,a_{N}),

and assume that
\re(a_{N})>0
(
<0
is the same proof by considering
t\to-\infty)
. If
\textbf{f}=(f_{1},\dots,f_{N})^{t}
are the coordinate functions of the embedding
\mathscr{C}\to\mathbb{C}^{N}
, then

A\exp(tD)\textbf{f}=\diag(e^{a_{1}t}\dots,e^{a_{N}t})A\textbf{f}.

Therefore, for some
b_{1},\dots,b_{N}
not all zero and determined by
A
, the last coordinate of
A\exp(tD)\textbf{f}
is
e^{a_{N}t}(b_{1}f_{1}+\cdots+b_{N}f_{N})
. As
t\to\infty
, then
|e^{a_{N}t}(b_{1}f_{1}+\cdots+b_{N}f_{N})|\to\infty
. As all
f_{1},\dots,f_{N}
are linearly independent, then some
f_{i}\to 0
with
b_{i}\neq 0
, contradicting
\exp(tD)
having compact orbits. We conclude that
D\in\mathfrak{u}(N)
. Furthermore, the flow of
K
is polynomial and cannot only have compact orbits unless
K=0
. It follows that
JV\in\mathfrak{u}(N)
and hence
JV\in J\mathfrak{p}
, i.e.
JV
is a Killing field for
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
.

Finally, in all of the above arguments, we can interchange the roles of
(\omega_{\mathscr{C}},\xi)
and
(\omega_{\infty},JV)
, i.e. consider
\omega_{\infty}
as the reference metric and apply the decomposition in Theorem
2.26
to
\xi
. This shows the second part of the proposition.
∎

2.3.3.
Deformations of the Sasakian Structure

Recall that the Sasakian manifold associated with
(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
is the compact manifold
L
equipped with a tuple
(g_{L},\eta,\xi,\Phi)
satisfying the following conditions:
g_{L}
is the cone metric
g_{\mathscr{C}}
on
\mathscr{C}
pulled back to
\{r=1\}\cong L
, and
\xi
is the associated Reeb field parallel to
\{r=1\}
.
\eta
is the contact form defined by
\eta(\cdot)=\frac{1}{r^{2}}g_{L}(\xi,\cdot)
, and
\Phi
is an endomorphism of
TL
given by
\Phi(X)=J(X-\eta(X)\xi)
. Since
\xi
and
JV
commute and are Reeb fields associated with some Kähler cone metrics, the combined flow generates a torus
\mathbb{T}
that acts by holomorphic isometries on
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
and
\omega_{\infty}
. It is important to note that the torus
\mathbb{T}
generated by
\xi
and
JV
preserves
(g_{L},\eta,\xi,\Phi)
. As the associated
(1,1)
-form
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
is a symplectic form, the action by
\mathbb{T}
is symplectic on
(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
and generates a moment map

(2.25)

\mu\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathfrak{t}^{*},

where
\mathfrak{t}^{*}
is the dual of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{t}
of
\mathbb{T}
. By
[
50
, p. 18]
and in terms of
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
and
p=(r,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times L
, the map is given by
\mu(p)(X)=r^{2}(\omega_{\mathscr{C}})_{x}(\xi,X)
, where
X\in\mathfrak{t}
is regarded as the induced vector field on
\mathscr{C}
by the action
\mathbb{T}\times\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}
. According to
[
17
, Theorem 1]
, the image of
\mathscr{C}
under
\mu
is a strongly convex rational polyhedral cone
\mathcal{C}^{*}\subset\mathfrak{t}^{*}
. Denote the interior of the dual cone by
\mathcal{C}_{0}\subset\mathfrak{t}
. It is worth noticing that
\xi\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
since
\eta(\xi)=1
, but we do not know if
JV\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
a priori. The goal of this section is to prove this.

In doing so, the transverse Kähler deformations must be defined. As the name suggests, they are deformations along the transverse structure obtained by the quotient of the Reeb field
\xi
on
L
and thus leave
\xi
invariant. They have the property of leaving the cone
\mathscr{C}_{0}
invariant and are given in the following lemma:

Lemma 2.29

(
[
50
, Lemma 7]
)
.

The space of all Sasakian structures with Reeb field
\xi
and transverse complex structure
J
is an affine space modeled on
(C_{b}^{\infty}(L)/\mathbb{R})\times(C_{b}^{\infty}(L)/\mathbb{R})
(
C_{b}^{\infty}
denotes basic functions). Given a Sasakian structure
(L,g_{L},\xi,\eta,\Phi)
, a new structure
(L,\tilde{g}_{L},\xi,\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\Phi})
with the same Reeb vector field
\xi
is obtained by

\displaystyle\tilde{\eta}

\displaystyle=\eta+d^{c}\varphi+d\psi,

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}

\displaystyle=\Phi-\xi\otimes\tilde{\eta}\circ\Phi,

\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{L}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}d\tilde{\eta}\circ(\Id\otimes\tilde{\Phi})+\tilde{\eta}\otimes\tilde{\eta},

\displaystyle\tilde{\omega}_{L}

\displaystyle=\omega_{L}+\frac{1}{2}dd^{c}\varphi,

where
\varphi
and
\psi
are two basic smooth functions.

Proposition 2.30
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
and
(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\infty})
be two Kähler cone metrics with commuting Reeb fields
\xi
and
JV
. Let
\mathbb{T}
denote the torus generated by the flows of
\xi
and
JV
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{t}
, and let
\mathcal{C}_{0}
be the interior of the dual cone for
(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
. Then
JV\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
.

Proof.

First, embed
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
and
\omega_{\infty}
into
\mathbb{C}^{N}
via the embedding from Lemma
2.27
with the torus-action
\mathbb{T}
coming from
\xi
and
JV
. On
\mathbb{C}^{N}
, the scaling vector fields
r\partial_{r}
and
V
are given by

(2.26)

r\partial_{r}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(a_{i}z_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}+a_{i}\overline{z_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z_{i}}}\right),\quad V=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(b_{i}z_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}+b_{i}\overline{z_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z_{i}}}\right),

for
a_{i},b_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
. As the functions
(f_{j})
are eigenfunctions of the torus-action
\mathbb{T}
, they are also eigenfunctions of the complexified torus
\mathbb{T}_{\mathbb{C}}
, which includes the action of the scaling vector fields
r\partial_{r}
and
V
. As the flow of the scaling vector fields transports any point towards
o
on
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
as
t\to-\infty
, it follows that
a_{i},b_{i}>0
for all
i=1,\dots,N
. We may therefore construct Kähler metrics
\tilde{\omega}_{0}
and
\tilde{\omega}_{1}
on
\mathbb{C}^{N}
with the same Reeb vector fields
\xi_{0}=\xi
and
\xi_{1}=JV
and invariant under
\U(1)^{N}
:

\displaystyle\tilde{\omega}_{0}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{i}{2}\partial\overline{\partial}(|z_{1}|^{2a_{1}^{-1}}+\cdots+|z_{N}|^{2a_{N}^{-1}}),\quad\xi_{0}=\xi,

\displaystyle\tilde{\omega}_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{i}{2}\partial\overline{\partial}(|z_{1}|^{2b_{1}^{-1}}+\cdots+|z_{N}|^{2b_{N}^{-1}}),\quad\xi_{1}=JV.

Restrict
\tilde{\omega}_{0}
and
\tilde{\omega}_{1}
to
\mathscr{C}\subset\mathbb{C}^{N}
. As
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
and
\tilde{\omega}_{0}
(resp.
\omega_{\infty}
and
\tilde{\omega}_{1}
) have the same Reeb field and transverse complex structure, they are transverse Kähler deformations of one another, and so have the same dual cone
\mathcal{C}_{0}

[
50
, p. 18]
. By
[
50
, eq. 45]
, to show that
JV\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
, it is enough to show that

\tilde{\omega}_{0}(J(r\partial_{r}),V)=\tilde{g}_{0}(r\partial_{r},V)>0,

where
\tilde{g}_{0}
is the Riemannian metric associated with
\tilde{\omega}_{0}
. But this is shown by direct evaluation:

\tilde{g}_{0}(r\partial_{r},V)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}a_{i}^{-1}b_{i}|z_{i}|^{2a_{i}^{-1}}>0.

The same argument shows that
\xi\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
.
∎

2.4.
The Tangent Cones equal
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}

The previous section proved that the dual moment cone
\mathcal{C}_{0}
of
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
contains
\xi
and
JV
, both of which are associated with Sasaki-Einstein metrics. Therefore, the results in
[
35
]
will show that
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
and
\omega_{\infty}
are equal up to pullback by an element in
\Aut_{\Scl}(\mathscr{C})
. To prove this, we first set up the necessary notation.

Lemma 2.31
.

There exists a holomorphic
(m,0)
-form
\Omega_{\mathscr{C}}
such that the Reeb fields
\xi
and
JV
of
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
and
\omega_{\infty}
, respectively, satisfy
\mathscr{L}_{\xi}\Omega_{\mathscr{C}}=\mathscr{L}_{JV}\Omega_{\mathscr{C}}=im\Omega_{\mathscr{C}}
.

Proof.

As both
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
and
\omega_{\infty}
are Ricci-flat Kähler cone metrics on the Calabi-Yau cone
\mathscr{C}
, there exist
\Omega_{\mathscr{C}}
and
\Omega_{\infty}
such that
\mathscr{L}_{JV}\Omega_{\infty}=im\Omega_{\infty}
and
\mathscr{L}_{\xi}\Omega_{\mathscr{C}}=im\Omega_{\mathscr{C}}

[
35
, p. 624]
. These have the property

\omega_{\mathscr{C}}^{m}=i^{m}(-1)^{m(m-1)/2}\Omega_{\mathscr{C}}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{\mathscr{C}},\quad\omega_{\infty}^{m}=i^{m}(-1)^{m(m-1)/2}\Omega_{\infty}\wedge\overline{\Omega}_{\infty}.

Using the fact that
\Omega_{\mathscr{C}}
and
\Omega_{\infty}
are both holomorphic sections of the line bundle
\bigwedge^{(m,0)}(\mathscr{C})
, there is a holomorphic function
h\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}
such that

\Omega_{\infty}=h\Omega_{\mathscr{C}}.

By the volume condition
\omega_{\infty}^{m}=\omega_{\mathscr{C}}^{m}
, we have

i^{-m}(-1)^{-m(m-1)/2}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}^{m}=\Omega_{\mathscr{C}}\wedge\bar{\Omega}_{\mathscr{C}}=\Omega_{\infty}\wedge\bar{\Omega}_{\infty}=|h|^{2}\Omega_{\mathscr{C}}\wedge\bar{\Omega}_{\mathscr{C}}.

Since
h
is a mapping
\mathscr{C}\to S^{1}
and is holomorphic,
h
is a constant. The lemma follows.
∎

Definition 2.32

(
[
35
, p. 624]
)
.

Given the cone metric
(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
with a torus
\mathbb{T}
acting by holomorphic isometries, define the set

\Sigma_{\mathscr{C}}=\{\tilde{\xi}\in\mathcal{C}_{0}\,|\,\mathscr{L}_{\tilde{\xi}}\Omega_{\mathscr{C}}=im\Omega_{\mathscr{C}}\}.

Proposition 2.33

(
[
35
, p. 632]
)
.

\Sigma_{\mathscr{C}}
is convex.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{\xi},\tilde{\xi}^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{\mathscr{C}}
. Then there exists
Y\in\mathfrak{t}
such that
\tilde{\xi}^{\prime}=\tilde{\xi}+Y
and

\mathscr{L}_{Y}\Omega_{\mathscr{C}}=0.

The space of all
Y\in\mathfrak{t}
for which
\mathscr{L}_{Y}\Omega_{\mathscr{C}}=0
forms a vector space in
\mathfrak{t}
. Thus,
\Sigma_{\mathscr{C}}
is an affine space intersected with the convex cone
\mathcal{C}_{0}
and hence also convex.
∎

The following theorem is crucial in showing that
\xi=JV
.

Theorem 2.34

(
[
35
, p. 635 and p. 638]
)
.

Define the volume function
