\frac{\varrho_{i}}{\delta_{i}}\to 0
: Blow up by
\delta_{i}^{-1}
such that the maximizing ball of the seminorm has distance 1 to the apex:

•

x_{i}^{\sharp}\coloneqq\Phi_{\delta_{i}}^{-1}(x_{i})
,

•

\delta_{i}^{-2}\Phi_{\delta_{i}}^{*}(\omega_{\mathscr{C}})=\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
,

•

\omega_{i}^{\sharp}\coloneqq\delta_{i}^{-2}\Phi_{\delta_{i}}^{*}(\tilde{\omega}_{i})
,

•

\varrho_{i}^{\sharp}\coloneqq\frac{\varrho_{i}}{\delta_{i}}
,
\nu_{i}^{\sharp}\coloneqq\frac{\nu_{i}}{\delta_{i}}<1
,
\delta_{i}^{\sharp}\coloneqq\frac{\delta_{i}}{\delta_{i}}=1
,

•

\Phi_{\delta_{i}}^{*}(\tilde{f}_{i})\coloneqq f_{i}^{\sharp}
.

By assumption,
\varrho_{i}^{\sharp}\to 0
as
i\to\infty
, while
1=\delta_{i}^{\sharp}=\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(o,B_{\varrho_{i}^{\sharp}}(x_{i}^{\sharp}))
. The blowup by
\delta_{i}^{-1}
also scales the seminorm (
3.34
):

(3.38)

[\omega_{i}^{\sharp}]_{\alpha,B_{2(\delta_{i}\epsilon_{i})^{-1}}(o),f_{i}^{\sharp},\Sigma_{3C}^{2}\times\Sigma_{\loc}^{2}}^{\prime}=\delta_{i}^{\alpha},

with the ball
B_{\varrho_{i}^{\sharp}}(x_{i}^{\sharp})
realizing at least half of the seminorm in (
3.38
). Let
\pi_{i}^{\sharp}\in\Sigma_{3C}^{2}
be the minimizing element in (
3.38
) on
B_{3}(o)\setminus B_{\frac{1}{4}}(o)
. As
B_{3}(o)\setminus B_{\frac{1}{4}}(o)
is not contained in any coordinate ball
U
, there is no contribution from
\Sigma_{\loc}^{2}
. (
3.38
) shows that:

(3.39)

\left\lVert\omega_{i}^{\sharp}-\pi_{i}^{\sharp}\right\rVert_{0,B_{3}(o)\setminus B_{\frac{1}{4}}(o)}\leqslant 3^{\alpha}f_{i}^{\sharp}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{-1}\delta_{i}^{\alpha},

where
3^{\alpha}f_{i}^{\sharp}(\frac{1}{4})^{-1}\leqslant C<\infty
uniformly by the condition
f_{i}^{\sharp}(1)=1
. By Lemma
3.9
and Lemma
3.14
, then

\displaystyle\left\lVert\omega_{i}^{\sharp}-\pi_{i}^{\sharp}\right\rVert_{0,\beta,B_{2}(o)\setminus B_{\frac{1}{2}}(o),\Sigma_{\loc}^{2}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leqslant C^{\prime}\left\lVert\omega_{i}^{\sharp}-\pi_{i}^{\sharp}\right\rVert_{0,\beta,B_{2}(o)\setminus B_{\frac{1}{2}}(o)}

\displaystyle\leqslant C^{\prime}\left\lVert\omega_{i}^{\sharp}-\pi_{i}^{\sharp}\right\rVert_{0,B_{3}(o)\setminus B_{\frac{1}{4}}(o)}

\displaystyle\leqslant C^{\prime}\delta_{i}^{\alpha}

for all
\beta<1
and
C^{\prime}
depending on
\beta
.

As
\varrho_{i}^{\sharp}\to 0
, all
B_{\varrho_{i}^{\sharp}}(x_{i}^{\sharp})
are contained in
B_{2}(o)\setminus B_{\frac{1}{2}}(o)
for
i
sufficiently large. Hence, on
B_{2}(o)\setminus B_{\frac{1}{2}}(o)
, we proceed as in (
3.37
):

\displaystyle\frac{\delta_{i}^{\alpha}}{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant(\varrho_{i}^{\sharp})^{-\alpha}\min_{(\pi,\eta)\in\Sigma_{3C}^{2}\times\Sigma_{\loc}^{2}}\left\lVert\omega_{i}^{\sharp}-\pi-\eta\right\rVert_{0,B_{\varrho_{i}^{\sharp}}(x_{i}^{\sharp})}

\displaystyle\leqslant(\varrho_{i}^{\sharp})^{-\alpha}\min_{\eta\in\Sigma_{\loc}^{2}}\left\lVert\omega_{i}^{\sharp}-\pi_{i}^{\sharp}-\eta\right\rVert_{0,B_{\varrho_{i}^{\sharp}}(x_{i}^{\sharp})}

\displaystyle=(\varrho_{i}^{\sharp})^{\beta-\alpha}(\varrho_{i}^{\sharp})^{-\beta}\min_{\eta\in\Sigma_{\loc}^{2}}\left\lVert\omega_{i}^{\sharp}-\pi_{i}^{\sharp}-\eta\right\rVert_{0,B_{\varrho_{i}^{\sharp}}(x_{i}^{\sharp})}

\displaystyle\leqslant(\varrho_{i}^{\sharp})^{\beta-\alpha}C^{\prime}\delta_{i}^{\alpha}.

As
\alpha<1
and if we pick
\beta>\alpha
, then:

\frac{1}{2}\leqslant C^{\prime}(\varrho_{i}^{\sharp})^{\beta-\alpha}\to 0,

a clear contradiction.

From the above three cases, (
3.36
) follows.

3.5.8.
Case 3:

\varrho_{i}\to 0
. Linearizing the Complex Monge-Ampère Equation and Proof

First, we rescale
\tilde{\omega}_{i}
such that
\varrho_{i}
equals 1 in the rescaled coordinates, denoted by
\hat{\cdot}
. More precisely, define:

•

\hat{x}_{i}\coloneqq\Phi_{\varrho_{i}}^{-1}(x_{i})
,

•

\varrho_{i}^{-2}\Phi_{\varrho_{i}}^{*}(\omega_{\mathscr{C}})=\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
,

•

\hat{\omega}_{i}\coloneqq\varrho_{i}^{-2}\Phi_{\varrho_{i}}^{*}(\tilde{\omega}_{i})
,

•

\hat{\varrho}_{i}\coloneqq\frac{\varrho_{i}}{\varrho_{i}}=1
,
\hat{\nu}_{i}\coloneqq\frac{\nu_{i}}{\varrho_{i}}<1
,
\hat{\delta}_{i}\coloneqq\frac{\delta_{i}}{\varrho_{i}}\leqslant A
,

•

\hat{f}_{i}\coloneqq\Phi_{\varrho_{i}}^{*}(\tilde{f}_{i})
.

\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
is invariant under this rescaling as it is a cone metric. The rescaling has the effect of decreasing the
{C^{0,\alpha}}^{\prime}
-seminorm:

(3.40)

[\hat{\omega}_{i}]_{\alpha,B_{2(\epsilon_{i}\varrho_{i})^{-1}}(o),\hat{f}_{i},\Sigma_{3C}^{2}\times\Sigma_{\loc}^{2}}^{\prime}=\varrho_{i}^{\alpha}[\tilde{\omega}_{i}]_{\alpha,B_{2(\epsilon_{i})^{-1}}(o),\tilde{f}_{i},\Sigma_{3C}^{2}\times\Sigma_{\loc}^{2}}^{\prime}=\varrho_{i}^{\alpha}.

We know that
\hat{\omega}_{i}\to\hat{\omega}_{\infty}
in
C_{\loc}^{1,\beta}(\mathscr{C})
for some
\hat{\omega}_{\infty}=\hat{\Psi}^{*}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
, but the rate of convergence is not known. Notice that by (
3.36
), then
\hat{\delta}_{i}\leqslant A<\infty
. The proof proceeds by modifying
\hat{\omega}_{i}
to
\check{\omega}_{i}
with a nonvanishing primed seminorm. First, we subtract an element
\pi_{i}\in\Sigma_{3C}^{2}
such that we obtain uniform
C^{0,\beta}_{\loc}
-estimates of
\varrho_{i}^{-\alpha}(\hat{\omega}_{i}-\pi_{i})
for any
\beta<1
. For this, pick
\pi_{i}^{R,\nu}\in\Sigma_{3C}^{2}
realizing (
3.40
) on
B_{R}(o)\setminus B_{\nu}(o)
, i.e.,

\left\lVert\hat{\omega}_{i}-\pi_{i}^{R,\nu}\right\rVert_{0,B_{R}(o)\setminus B_{\nu}(o)}\leqslant R^{\alpha}\hat{f}_{i}(\nu)^{-1}\varrho_{i}^{\alpha},

where
\hat{f}_{i}(\nu)^{-1}
has an upper bound because
\hat{\delta}_{i}\leqslant A
uniformly. Set
\pi_{i}\coloneqq\pi_{i}^{2,1}
. Then (
3.40
) implies:

(3.41)

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{i}(\nu)\left\lVert\hat{\omega}_{i}-\pi_{i}\right\rVert_{B_{R}(o)\setminus B_{\nu}(o)}

\displaystyle\leqslant\hat{f}_{i}(\nu)\left\lVert\hat{\omega}_{i}-\pi_{i}^{R,\nu}\right\rVert_{B_{R}(o)\setminus B_{\nu}(o)}+\hat{f}_{i}(\nu)\left\lVert\pi_{i}^{R,\nu}-\pi_{i}\right\rVert_{B_{R}(o)\setminus B_{\nu}(o)}

\displaystyle\leqslant R^{\alpha}\varrho_{i}^{\alpha}+\hat{f}_{i}(\nu)\left\lVert\pi_{i}^{R,\nu}-\pi_{i}\right\rVert_{B_{2}(o)\setminus B_{1}(o)}

\displaystyle\leqslant R^{\alpha}\varrho_{i}^{\alpha}+\hat{f}_{i}(\nu)\left\lVert\pi_{i}^{R,\nu}-\hat{\omega}_{i}\right\rVert_{B_{2}(o)\setminus B_{1}(o)}+\hat{f}_{i}(\nu)\left\lVert\hat{\omega}_{i}-\pi_{i}\right\rVert_{B_{2}(o)\setminus B_{1}(o)}

\displaystyle\leqslant(2R^{\alpha}+2)\varrho_{i}^{\alpha}.

Notice that
\left\lVert\pi_{i}^{R,\nu}-\pi_{i}\right\rVert_{B_{R}(o)\setminus B_{\nu}(o)}=\left\lVert\pi_{i}^{R,\nu}-\pi_{i}\right\rVert_{B_{2}(o)\setminus B_{1}(o)}
as
\pi_{i}^{R,\nu},\pi_{i}\in\Sigma_{3C}^{2}
are invariant under scaling. Hence, by Lemma
3.9
, on any compact set
K\Subset\mathscr{C}
and for any
\beta<1
, there exists a
D_{K}>0
such that

(3.42)

\left\lVert\hat{\omega}_{i}-\pi_{i}\right\rVert_{0,\beta,K}\leqslant D_{K}\varrho_{i}^{\alpha}.

Given this, define
\check{\omega}_{i}\coloneqq\varrho_{i}^{-\alpha}(\hat{\omega}_{i}-\pi_{i})
with

(3.43)

[\check{\omega}_{i}]^{\prime}_{\alpha,B_{2(\epsilon_{i}\varrho_{i})^{-1}}(o),\hat{f}_{i},(\varrho_{i}^{-\alpha}(\Sigma_{3C}^{2}-\pi_{i}))\times\Sigma_{\loc}^{2}}=1.

Notice the new comparison set incorporating
\pi_{i}
and multiplication by
\varrho_{i}^{-\alpha}
, defined as

\varrho_{i}^{-\alpha}(\Sigma_{3C}^{2}-\pi_{i})\coloneqq\{\varrho_{i}^{-\alpha}(\Psi^{*}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}-\pi_{i})\in\Omega^{2}(\mathscr{C})\mid\Psi^{*}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}\in\Sigma_{3C}^{2}\}.

As
\hat{f}_{i}(\delta_{i})=1
and
\hat{\delta}_{i}<A<\infty
for some
A>0
, the decay condition of
\hat{f}_{i}
and (
3.41
) show that

(3.44)

|\check{\omega}_{i}|\leqslant C\begin{cases}r^{-\frac{1+\alpha}{2}}&r\leqslant 1,\\
r^{\alpha}&r>1.\end{cases}

If
\hat{\delta}_{i}\to 0
, then
r^{-\frac{1+\alpha}{2}}
gets replaced by the constant function
1
in the limit. (
3.42
) shows that
\check{\omega}_{i}\in C^{0,\beta}(K)
for any compact set
K\Subset\mathscr{C}
.

To obtain a differential equation for
\check{\omega}_{i}
, rewrite
\hat{\omega}_{i}^{m}=\frac{\hat{\omega}_{i}^{m}}{\pi_{i}^{m}}\pi_{i}^{m}
as

(3.45)

\displaystyle\frac{\hat{\omega}_{i}^{m}}{\pi_{i}^{m}}\pi_{i}^{m}

\displaystyle=\hat{\omega}_{i}^{m}=\varrho_{i}^{\alpha\cdot m}(\varrho_{i}^{-\alpha}(\hat{\omega}_{i}-\pi_{i})+\varrho_{i}^{-\alpha}\pi_{i})^{m}

\displaystyle=\varrho_{i}^{\alpha\cdot m}(\check{\omega}_{i}+\varrho_{i}^{-\alpha}\pi_{i})^{m}

\displaystyle=\pi_{i}^{m}+m\varrho_{i}^{\alpha}\pi_{i}^{m-1}\wedge\check{\omega}_{i}+\mathcal{O}(\varrho_{i}^{2\alpha}).

Subtracting
\pi_{i}^{m}
and dividing by
\varrho_{i}^{\alpha}\pi_{i}^{m}
implies that

(3.46)

\displaystyle m\tr_{\pi_{i}}\check{\omega}_{i}+\mathcal{O}(\varrho_{i}^{\alpha})=\varrho_{i}^{-\alpha}\left(\frac{\hat{\omega}_{i}^{m}}{\pi_{i}^{m}}-1\right).

On the left-hand side,
\tr_{\pi_{i}}\check{\omega}_{i}
is controlled in
C^{0,\beta}_{\loc}(\mathscr{C})
, and all terms in
\mathcal{O}(\varrho_{i}^{\alpha})
converge to
0
in
C^{0,\beta}_{\loc}(\mathscr{C})
as
\check{\omega}_{i}
is controlled in
C^{0,\beta}_{\loc}(\mathscr{C})
for
i
big enough due to (
3.42
). On the right-hand side, write

\omega_{i}^{m}=e^{G_{i}}\pi_{i}^{m},\quad\hat{\omega}_{i}^{m}=e^{\hat{G}_{i}}\pi_{i}^{m}.

(
3.46
) becomes:

(3.47)

m\tr_{\pi_{i}}\check{\omega}_{i}=\varrho_{i}^{-\alpha}\left(e^{\hat{G}_{i}}-1\right)+\mathcal{O}(\varrho_{i}^{\alpha}).

The blowups by
(\epsilon_{i})
and
(\varrho_{i})
imply, by the uniform bound (
3.28
), Ricci-flatness of
\pi_{i}
, and Proposition
3.5
, that

(3.48)

[e^{\hat{G}_{i}}]_{0,\beta,B_{2(\epsilon_{i}\varrho_{i})^{-1}}(o)}\leqslant C_{3}\epsilon_{i}^{\beta}\varrho_{i}^{\beta}.

As the right-hand side of (
3.46
) is bounded in
C^{0,\beta}_{\loc}(\mathscr{C})
, (
3.48
) shows that the right-hand side of (
3.46
) converges to a constant in
C^{0,\beta}_{\loc}(\mathscr{C})
as
i\to\infty
.

Taking
i\to\infty
, we obtain a limit
\check{\omega}_{\infty}
in
C^{0,\beta}_{\loc}(\mathscr{C})
, satisfying

(3.49)

\tr_{\hat{\omega}_{\infty}}\check{\omega}_{\infty}=\text{constant},

and with
\pi_{i}\to\hat{\omega}_{\infty}
in
C^{\infty}_{\loc}(\mathscr{C})
.
\check{\omega}_{\infty}
also satisfies the growth condition (
3.44
), so as
-\frac{1+\alpha}{2}>-1
and
\alpha
is very small, Proposition
C.5
shows that
\mathscr{L}_{r\partial_{r}}\check{\omega}_{\infty}=2\check{\omega}_{\infty}
and
\check{\omega}_{\infty}=i\partial\overline{\partial}(cr^{2}+u)
for some constant
c
and 2-homogeneous,
\xi
-invariant, harmonic
u
.

To finally show the claim, we consider the following two cases:

Subcase 3.
1)

\hat{\delta}_{i}>a>0
for some
a>0
. Then:

(3.50)

\frac{1}{2}\leqslant\min_{\theta\in\varrho_{i}^{-\alpha}(\Sigma_{3C}^{2}-\pi_{i})}\hat{f}_{i}(\hat{\delta}_{i})\left\lVert\check{\omega}_{i}-\theta\right\rVert_{0,B_{1}(\hat{x}_{i})\setminus B_{\hat{\nu}_{i}}(\hat{x}_{i})}\leqslant\min_{\theta\in\varrho_{i}^{-\alpha}(\Sigma_{3C}^{2}-\pi_{i})}\left\lVert\check{\omega}_{i}-\theta\right\rVert_{0,K},

for some compact set
K\Subset\mathscr{C}
containing all
B_{1}(\hat{x}_{i})\setminus B_{\hat{\nu}_{i}}(\hat{x}_{i})
. Furthermore,

\left\lVert\check{\omega}_{i}-\check{\omega}_{\infty}\right\rVert_{0,K}\to 0,\quad i\to\infty,

where, by Corollary
3.12
, for any
\mu>0
and
i
big enough, there is an element
\theta_{i}\in\varrho_{i}^{-\alpha}(\Sigma_{3C}^{2}-\pi_{i})
such that

\left\lVert\check{\omega}_{\infty}-\theta_{i}\right\rVert_{0,K}\leqslant\mu,

showing the claim in this case by the triangle inequality.

Subcase 3.
2)

\hat{\delta}_{i}\to 0
: We use the above arguments combined with the Hölder bound of (
3.43
) to extend the proof down to
o
as in subcase 2.2. The proof is almost the same. As before,
B_{1}(\hat{x}_{i})\setminus B_{\hat{\nu}_{i}}(\hat{x}_{i})
leaves every compact set in
\mathscr{C}
, and so we cannot use the convergence
\check{\omega}_{i}\to\check{\omega}_{\infty}
in
C^{0,\beta}_{\loc}(\mathscr{C})
directly.

As
\check{\omega}_{i}\to\check{\omega}_{\infty}
in
C_{\loc}^{0,\alpha}(\mathscr{C})
, then for any small but fixed
\mu>0
and
i
big enough:

(3.51)

\left\lVert\check{\omega}_{i}-\check{\omega}_{\infty}\right\rVert_{0,[\mu,2]\times L}\leqslant\mu.

By the Hölder bound (
3.43
) and choosing the set
[\hat{\delta}_{i},2\mu]\times L
in the seminorm, there exists
\psi_{i}\in\varrho_{i}^{-\alpha}(\Sigma_{3C}^{2}-\pi_{i})
such that

\hat{f}_{i}(\hat{\delta}_{i})\left\lVert\check{\omega}_{i}-\psi_{i}\right\rVert_{0,[\hat{\delta}_{i},2\mu]\times L}\leqslant(2\mu)^{\alpha}.

The triangle inequality now implies that

(3.52)

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{i}(\hat{\delta}_{i})\left\lVert\check{\omega}_{\infty}-\psi_{i}\right\rVert_{0,[\mu,2\mu]\times L}

\displaystyle=\hat{f}_{i}(\hat{\delta}_{i})\left\lVert\check{\omega}_{\infty}-\check{\omega}_{i}+\check{\omega}_{i}-\psi_{i}\right\rVert_{0,[\mu,2\mu]\times L}

\displaystyle\leqslant\hat{f}_{i}(\hat{\delta}_{i})\left\lVert\check{\omega}_{\infty}-\check{\omega}_{i}\right\rVert_{0,[\mu,2\mu]\times L}+\hat{f}_{i}(\hat{\delta}_{i})\left\lVert\check{\omega}_{i}-\psi_{i}\right\rVert_{0,[\mu,2\mu]\times L}

\displaystyle\leqslant\mu+(2\mu)^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leqslant 3\mu^{\alpha}.

|\check{\omega}_{\infty}-\psi_{i}|_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}
is invariant under scaling, so it follows that

\hat{f}_{i}(\hat{\delta}_{i})\left\lVert\check{\omega}_{\infty}-\psi_{i}\right\rVert_{0,(0,2]\times L}\leqslant 3\mu^{\alpha}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{i}(\hat{\delta}_{i})\left\lVert\check{\omega}_{i}-\psi_{i}\right\rVert_{0,B_{1}(\hat{x}_{i})\setminus B_{\hat{\nu}_{i}}(\hat{x}_{i})}

\displaystyle\leqslant\hat{f}_{i}(\hat{\delta}_{i})\left\lVert\check{\omega}_{i}-\psi_{i}\right\rVert_{0,[\hat{\delta}_{i},2\mu]\times L}+\hat{f}_{i}(\hat{\delta}_{i})\left\lVert\check{\omega}_{i}-\psi_{i}\right\rVert_{0,[2\mu,2]\times L}

\displaystyle\leqslant(2\mu)^{\alpha}+\hat{f}_{i}(\hat{\delta}_{i})\left\lVert\check{\omega}_{i}-\check{\omega}_{\infty}+\check{\omega}_{\infty}-\psi_{i}\right\rVert_{0,[2\mu,2]\times L}

\displaystyle\leqslant(2\mu)^{\alpha}+\hat{f}_{i}(\hat{\delta}_{i})\left\lVert\check{\omega}_{i}-\check{\omega}_{\infty}\right\rVert_{0,[2\mu,2]\times L}+\hat{f}_{i}(\hat{\delta}_{i})\left\lVert\check{\omega}_{\infty}-\psi_{i}\right\rVert_{0,[2\mu,2]\times L}

\displaystyle\leqslant(2\mu)^{\alpha}+\mu+3\mu^{\alpha}

showing the claim in this case by choosing
\mu>0
small enough.

3.6.
Asymptotics of the Kähler Metric at
o

The following corollary follows from Theorem
3.15
:

Corollary 3.16

(Corollary
C
)
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
be a Calabi-Yau cone with cone metric
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
, and let
\omega
be a Kähler metric on
B_{3}(o)\subset\mathscr{C}
such that

(3.53)

\frac{1}{C}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}\leqslant\omega\leqslant C\omega_{\mathscr{C}},\quad\left\lVert\Scal(\omega)\right\rVert_{0,B_{3}(o)}\leqslant D,

for some constants
C,D>0
. Then, for any
\alpha>0
small enough, there exists an automorphism
\Psi\in\Aut_{\Scl}(\mathscr{C})
with

|\Psi^{*}\omega-\omega_{\mathscr{C}}|_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}\leqslant C^{\prime}r^{\alpha}

for
r\leqslant 1
.
\alpha=\alpha(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
and
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime}(\mathscr{C},C,D,\alpha,\omega_{\mathscr{C}},\mathfrak{U})
are independent of
\omega
.

If
\Scal(\omega)=0
, then

|\nabla_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}^{k}(\Psi^{*}\omega)-\omega_{\mathscr{C}}|_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}\leqslant C_{k}^{\prime}r^{\alpha-k},\qquad k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},

for
r\leqslant 1
and
C_{k}^{\prime}=C_{k}^{\prime}(\mathscr{C},C,D,k,\alpha,\omega_{\mathscr{C}},\mathfrak{U})

From Corollary
3.16
, one immediately concludes that the tangent cone at
o
of any
\omega
satisfying the requirements of Theorem
3.15
is unique.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.15
, there is a bound
[\omega]^{\prime}_{\alpha,B_{1}(o),\Sigma_{3C}^{2}\times\Sigma_{\loc}^{2}}\leqslant C^{\prime}
on the
{C^{0,\alpha}}^{\prime}
-seminorm. The seminorm contains a supremum over all points
x\in\overline{\mathscr{C}}
, so fix
x=o
as the apex. The only achievable elements in the comparison set lie in
\Sigma_{3C}^{2}
, so by Theorem
3.15
and for all
0<\varrho\leqslant 1
, there exists
\pi_{\varrho}\in\Sigma_{3C}^{2}
such that:

(3.54)

\left\lVert\omega-\pi_{\varrho}\right\rVert_{0,B_{\varrho}(o)}\leqslant C^{\prime}\varrho^{\alpha}.

As
\omega
is bounded,
(\pi_{\varrho})
is also bounded. Define the blowup
\tilde{\omega}_{\varrho}\coloneqq\varrho^{-2}\Phi_{\varrho}^{*}(\omega)
.
\pi_{\varrho}
is invariant under this rescaling as
\pi_{\varrho}\in\Sigma_{3C}^{2}
. Thus, (
3.54
) becomes:

\left\lVert\tilde{\omega}_{\varrho}-\pi_{\varrho}\right\rVert_{0,B_{1}(o)}\leqslant C^{\prime}\varrho^{\alpha}.

Proposition
2.8
and the Liouville Theorem (Theorem
2.3
) shows that there exists a
C^{1,\beta}_{\loc}(\mathscr{C})
-sublimit
\tilde{\omega}_{\varrho_{i}}\to\Psi^{*}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
for some
\Psi\in\Aut_{\Scl}(\mathscr{C})
and
\varrho_{i}\to 0
. The triangle inequality yields:

\left|\left\lVert\tilde{\omega}_{\varrho_{i}}-\Psi^{*}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}\right\rVert_{0,[\frac{1}{2},1]\times L}-\left\lVert\Psi^{*}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}-\pi_{\varrho_{i}}\right\rVert_{0,[\frac{1}{2},1]\times L}\right|\leqslant\left\lVert\tilde{\omega}_{\varrho_{i}}-\pi_{\varrho_{i}}\right\rVert_{0,[\frac{1}{2},1]\times L}\leqslant C^{\prime}\varrho_{i}^{\alpha}.

Since
\left\lVert\tilde{\omega}_{\varrho_{i}}-\Psi^{*}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}\right\rVert_{0,[\frac{1}{2},1]\times L}\to 0
as
i\to\infty
, we conclude that
\left\lVert\Psi^{*}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}-\pi_{\varrho_{i}}\right\rVert_{0,[\frac{1}{2},1]\times L}=\left\lVert\Psi^{*}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}-\pi_{\varrho_{i}}\right\rVert_{0,\mathscr{C}}\to 0
due to the
r\partial_{r}
-invariance of
\Psi^{*}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
and
\pi_{\varrho_{i}}
.

Claim:

\Psi^{*}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
satisfies:

(3.55)

\left\lVert\omega-\Psi^{*}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}\right\rVert_{0,B_{\varrho_{i}}(o)}\leqslant 4C^{\prime}\varrho_{i}^{\alpha}.

Proof:
For each
\varrho>0
, define the norm:

\left\lVert\omega\right\rVert_{\varrho}\coloneqq\varrho^{-\alpha}\left\lVert\omega\right\rVert_{0,B_{\varrho}(o)}.

In this notation, (
3.54
) reads:

(3.56)

\left\lVert\omega-\pi_{\varrho_{i}}\right\rVert_{\varrho_{i}}\leqslant C^{\prime}.

For fixed
k
, and in each norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\varrho_{k}}
, we know that
\pi_{\varrho_{i}}\to\Psi^{*}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
. Thus, for each
k
, there exists
i_{k}\geqslant k
with
\varrho_{i_{k}}\leqslant\varrho_{k}
such that:

\left\lVert\Psi^{*}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}-\pi_{\varrho_{i_{k}}}\right\rVert_{\varrho_{k}}\leqslant C^{\prime}.

Therefore:

(3.57)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\omega-\Psi^{*}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}\right\rVert_{\varrho_{k}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\lVert\omega-\pi_{\varrho_{k}}\right\rVert_{\varrho_{k}}+\left\lVert\pi_{\varrho_{k}}-\pi_{\varrho_{i_{k}}}\right\rVert_{\varrho_{k}}+\left\lVert\pi_{\varrho_{i_{k}}}-\Psi^{*}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}\right\rVert_{\varrho_{k}}

\displaystyle\leqslant C^{\prime}+\left\lVert\pi_{\varrho_{k}}-\pi_{\varrho_{i_{k}}}\right\rVert_{\varrho_{k}}+C^{\prime}.

Hence, to prove the claim, we only need to control
\left\lVert\pi_{\varrho_{k}}-\pi_{\varrho_{i_{k}}}\right\rVert_{\varrho_{k}}
. As
\pi_{\varrho_{k}}
and
\pi_{\varrho_{i_{k}}}
are invariant under
r\partial_{r}
, we can restrict the
C^{0}
-norm to
B_{\varrho_{i_{k}}}(o)\subset B_{\varrho_{k}}(o)
:

(3.58)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\pi_{\varrho_{k}}-\pi_{\varrho_{i_{k}}}\right\rVert_{\varrho_{k}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\varrho_{k}}{\varrho_{i_{k}}}\right)^{-\alpha}\left\lVert\pi_{\varrho_{k}}-\pi_{\varrho_{i_{k}}}\right\rVert_{\varrho_{i_{k}}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\frac{\varrho_{k}}{\varrho_{i_{k}}}\right)^{-\alpha}\left(\left\lVert\omega-\pi_{\varrho_{k}}\right\rVert_{\varrho_{i_{k}}}+\left\lVert\omega-\pi_{\varrho_{i_{k}}}\right\rVert_{\varrho_{i_{k}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\lVert\omega-\pi_{\varrho_{k}}\right\rVert_{\varrho_{k}}+\left(\frac{\varrho_{k}}{\varrho_{i_{k}}}\right)^{-\alpha}\left\lVert\omega-\pi_{\varrho_{i_{k}}}\right\rVert_{\varrho_{i_{k}}}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2C^{\prime},

by (
3.56
) and as
\left(\frac{\varrho_{k}}{\varrho_{i_{k}}}\right)^{-\alpha}\leqslant 1
. Combining (
3.57
) and (
3.58
) proves the claim.
\square

To show that (
3.55
) holds for any
0<\varrho\leqslant 1
, take another subsequence
\tilde{\varrho}_{i}\to 0
with sublimit
\tilde{\Psi}^{*}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
and consider:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Psi^{*}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}-\tilde{\Psi}^{*}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}\right\rVert_{0,\mathscr{C}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\lVert\Psi^{*}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}-\omega\right\rVert_{0,B_{\varrho_{i}}(o)}+\left\lVert\omega-\tilde{\Psi}^{*}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}\right\rVert_{0,B_{\tilde{\varrho}_{i}}(o)}

\displaystyle\leqslant 4C^{\prime}(\varrho_{i}^{\alpha}+\tilde{\varrho}_{i}^{\alpha}).

The first inequality is again due to the
r\partial_{r}
-invariance of
\Psi^{*}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
and
\tilde{\Psi}^{*}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
. Letting
i\to\infty
, it follows that
\Psi^{*}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}=\tilde{\Psi}^{*}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
, and hence proceeding by contradiction, (
3.55
) holds for any
\varrho>0
:

(3.59)

\left\lVert\omega-\Psi^{*}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}\right\rVert_{0,B_{\varrho}(o)}\leqslant 4C^{\prime}\varrho^{\alpha}.

A basic triangle inequality shows that
\frac{1}{2C}\leqslant\left\lVert\Psi^{*}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}\right\rVert_{0,\mathscr{C}}\leqslant 2C
, and Lemma
3.10
shows that this set is compact.
Hence, we can apply
\Psi^{-1}
to (
3.59
) and increase
C^{\prime}
such that:

\left\lVert(\Psi^{-1})^{*}\omega-\omega_{\mathscr{C}}\right\rVert_{0,B_{\varrho}(o)}\leqslant C^{\prime}\varrho^{\alpha},

with
C^{\prime}
independent of
\omega
. Redefine
\Psi^{-1}
to
\Psi
, and we have proved the corollary without derivatives.

For the rest of the proof, assume
\Psi=\Id
. To extend the estimate to derivatives, assume
\Scal(\omega)=0
and fix
p\in\mathscr{C}
with
\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(o,p)=R<\frac{1}{2}
. Pull back by
R^{-1}
such that
\tilde{p}\coloneqq\Phi_{R}^{*}(p)
with
\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(o,\tilde{p})=1
. Assume that
B_{\delta}(\tilde{p})
is trivial and pick holomorphic normal coordinates
z_{1}=x_{1}+ix_{2},\dots,z_{m}=x_{2m-1}+ix_{2m}
for
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
. Let
\tilde{\omega}=R^{-2}\Phi_{R}^{*}(\Psi^{*}\omega)
and write
\tilde{\omega}^{m}=e^{\tilde{F}}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}^{m}
. Then

\left\lVert\tilde{\omega}-\omega_{\mathscr{C}}\right\rVert_{0,B_{2}(o)}\leqslant C^{\prime}R^{\alpha}

by (
3.59
) and the assumption
\Psi=\Id
. The uniform bound implies that

\left\lVert e^{\tilde{F}}-1\right\rVert_{0,B_{2}(o)}\leqslant C^{\prime\prime}\left\lVert\omega-\omega_{\mathscr{C}}\right\rVert_{0,B_{2}(o)}\leqslant C^{\prime\prime}R^{\alpha},

for some constant
C^{\prime\prime}>0
. By the scalar-flatness of
\tilde{\omega}
and Ricci-flatness of
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
, then

(3.60)

\Delta_{\tilde{\omega}}\log e^{\tilde{F}}=0.

To estimate
|\log e^{\tilde{F}}|
, assume first that
e^{\tilde{F}}\geqslant 1
. Then the inequality
\log(1+x)\leqslant x
for
x\geqslant 0
implies that

\log e^{\tilde{F}}\leqslant e^{\tilde{F}}-1\leqslant C^{\prime\prime}R^{\alpha}.

The case
e^{\tilde{F}}<1
is similar, using
|\log(x)|=\log(x^{-1})
. By Lemma
3.9
:

\left\lVert\tilde{\omega}-\omega_{\mathscr{C}}\right\rVert_{0,\alpha,B_{\delta}(\tilde{p})}\leqslant C^{\prime\prime}R^{\alpha}.

Hence, using the Schauder estimates and bootstrapping, it follows that

\left\lVert e^{\tilde{F}}-1\right\rVert_{2,\alpha,B_{\frac{\delta}{2}}(\tilde{p})}\leqslant C^{\prime\prime}R^{\alpha}

for some constant
C^{\prime\prime}>0
.

Given the regularity of
e^{\tilde{F}}
, we upgrade the estimate to
\omega-\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
.

[
11
, Lemma 2.1]
shows that there exists a potential
\varphi\in C_{\loc}^{\infty}(B_{\frac{\delta}{2}}(\tilde{p}))
such that

i\partial\overline{\partial}\varphi=\tilde{\omega}-\omega_{\mathscr{C}},\quad\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{2,\alpha,B_{\frac{\delta}{2}}(\tilde{p})}\leqslant\left\lVert\tilde{\omega}-\omega_{\mathscr{C}}\right\rVert_{0,\alpha,B_{\delta}(\tilde{p})}<C^{\prime}R^{\alpha}.

For higher-order regularity, differentiate the complex Monge-Ampère equation:

\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\tilde{\omega}^{m}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}e^{\tilde{F}}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}^{m},

to find

(3.61)

m\Delta_{\tilde{\omega}}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x_{i}}=\frac{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}^{m}}{\tilde{\omega}^{m}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\left(e^{\tilde{F}}-1\right)+\frac{m}{\tilde{\omega}^{m}}\left(\left(e^{\tilde{F}}-1\right)\omega_{\mathscr{C}}^{m-1}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\omega_{\mathscr{C}}\right).

Due to the above estimates, the
C^{0,\alpha}(B_{\frac{\delta}{2}}(\tilde{p}))
-norm of the right-hand side is bounded by
CR^{\alpha}
for
R>0
small enough. Hence, the Schauder estimates imply that
\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{3,\alpha,B_{\frac{\delta}{3}}(\tilde{p})}\leqslant CR^{\alpha}
. Differentiating (
3.61
) and bootstrapping (
3.60
) and (
3.61
), we incrementally increase the regularity of
(e^{\tilde{F}}-1)
and
(\tilde{\omega}-\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
with
C^{k,\alpha}(B_{\frac{\delta}{4}}(\tilde{p}))
-bounds bounded by
R^{\alpha}
. We conclude that
\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{k,\alpha,B_{\frac{\delta}{4}}(\tilde{p})}\leqslant C_{k}R^{\alpha}
.
Covering
\{r=1\}
with finitely many such balls and scaling back implies the desired result:

|\nabla_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}^{k}((\Psi^{-1})^{*}\omega-\omega_{\mathscr{C}})|_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}\leqslant C_{k}R^{\alpha-k},

as
R\to 0
.
∎

Appendix A
Sobolev and Nash Inequalities on
{C(L)}

Proposition A.1
.

Let
({C(L)},g_{C(L)})
be a Riemannian cone of real dimension
n
with completion
\overline{C(L)}={C(L)}\cup\{o\}
and with the distance metric
\dist_{g_{{C(L)}}}
on
{C(L)}
extended to
\overline{C(L)}
. Let
g
be another metric uniformly equivalent to
{C(L)}
, i.e.,
\frac{1}{C^{\prime}}g_{C(L)}\leqslant g\leqslant C^{\prime}g_{C(L)}
. Then there exists a constant
C
such that

(A.1)

\left(\int_{B_{r}(p)}|u|^{\frac{2n}{n-2}}\dvol_{g}\right)^{\frac{n-2}{n}}\leqslant C\int_{B_{r}(p)}|\nabla_{g}u|_{g}^{2}\dvol_{g},

for
B_{r}(p)\subset{C(L)}
,
p\in\overline{C(L)}
,
0<r\leqslant\infty
, and
u\in C_{0}^{\infty}({C(L)})
.

Proof.

See
[
27
]
,
[
22
]
, and
[
36
]
for the method of proof. Take
u\in C_{0}^{\infty}(B_{r}(p))
. We assume
u\geqslant 0
as
|\nabla|u||\leqslant|\nabla u|
. By the uniform equivalence, it is enough to show (
A.1
) for
g_{C(L)}
and for
B_{r}(p)={C(L)}
(i.e.,
r=\infty
), as
C_{0}^{\infty}(B_{r^{\prime}}(p))\subset C_{0}^{\infty}({C(L)})
for any
r^{\prime}\leqslant\infty
. Let
A_{i}=[2^{i},2^{i+1}]\times L
for
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and define the average with respect to
g_{C(L)}
by

u_{i}=\fint_{A_{i}}u.

Then

\displaystyle\left(\int_{C(L)}u^{\frac{2n}{n-2}}\dvol_{g_{C(L)}}\right)^{\frac{n-2}{n}}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{A_{i}}u^{\frac{2n}{n-2}}\dvol_{g_{C(L)}}\right)^{\frac{n-2}{n}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\int_{A_{i}}u^{\frac{2n}{n-2}}\dvol_{g_{C(L)}}\right)^{\frac{n-2}{n}}

(A.2)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}2\left(\left(\int_{A_{i}}(u-u_{i})^{\frac{2n}{n-2}}\dvol_{g_{C(L)}}\right)^{\frac{n-2}{n}}+u_{i}^{2}|A_{i}|^{\frac{n-2}{n}}\right).

Using the Neumann-type Sobolev inequality on the first term (scaled up or down from
A_{0}
) on
g_{C(L)}
, then

\displaystyle\left(\int_{C(L)}u^{\frac{2n}{n-2}}\dvol_{g_{C(L)}}\right)^{\frac{n-2}{n}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(C\left(\int_{A_{i}}|\nabla_{g_{C(L)}}u|^{2}\dvol_{g_{C(L)}}\right)+2u_{i}^{2}|A_{i}|^{\frac{n-2}{n}}\right)

\displaystyle=C\int_{C(L)}|\nabla_{g_{C(L)}}u|^{2}\dvol_{g_{C(L)}}+2\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}u_{i}^{2}|A_{i}|^{\frac{n-2}{n}}.

For some large
K\in\mathbb{N}
not yet defined, expand the second term as

\displaystyle 2\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}u_{i}^{2}|A_{i}|^{\frac{n-2}{n}}

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}(u_{i}-u_{i+K})^{2}|A_{i}|^{\frac{n-2}{n}}+4\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}u_{i+K}^{2}|A_{i}|^{\frac{n-2}{n}}

\displaystyle=4\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}(u_{i}-u_{i+K})^{2}|A_{i}|^{\frac{n-2}{n}}+4\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\frac{|A_{i}|}{|A_{i+K}|}\right)^{\frac{n-2}{n}}u_{i+K}^{2}|A_{i+K}|^{\frac{n-2}{n}}.

As
|A_{i}|/|A_{i+K}|=2^{-nK}
, making
K
large means that the second term above can be absorbed into the left-hand side by increasing the constant 2. For the first term, one estimates each term in the sum as follows: define
B_{i,K}\coloneqq\left(\bigcup_{j=0}^{K}A_{i+j}\right)
, then

\displaystyle(u_{i}-u_{i+K})^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{|A_{i}|}\int_{A_{i}}u(x)\dvol_{g_{C(L)}}(x)-\frac{1}{|A_{i+K}|}\int_{A_{i+K}}u(y)\dvol_{g_{C(L)}}(y)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{|A_{i}||A_{i+k}|}\int_{A_{i}\times A_{i+K}}(u(x)-u(y))\dvol_{g_{C(L)}}^{2}(x,y)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{|A_{i}||A_{i+k}|}\int_{A_{i}\times A_{i+K}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{2}\dvol_{g_{C(L)}}^{2}(x,y)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{|A_{i}||A_{i+k}|}\int_{B_{i,K}\times B_{i,K}}|u(x)-u(y)|^{2}\dvol_{g_{C(L)}}^{2}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|A_{i}||A_{i+k}|}\int_{B_{i,K}\times B_{i,K}}|u(x)-u_{B_{i,K}}+u_{B_{i,K}}-u(y)|^{2}\dvol_{g_{C(L)}}^{2}(x,y)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{4|B_{i,K}|}{|A_{i}||A_{i+k}|}\int_{B_{i,K}}|u(x)-u_{B_{i,K}}|^{2}\dvol_{g_{C(L)}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{|B_{i,K}|}{|A_{i}||A_{i+k}|}C2^{2i}\int_{B_{i,K}}|\nabla_{g_{C(L)}}u|^{2}\dvol_{g_{C(L)}},

where
u_{B_{i,K}}
denotes the average of
u
over
B_{i,K}
. The last inequality is the Poincaré–Wirtinger inequality of
g_{C(L)}
applied to each
B_{i,K}
suitably scaled. Therefore, the first term in (
A.2
) becomes

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}(u_{i}-u_{i+K})^{2}|A_{i}|^{\frac{n-2}{n}}\leqslant C\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}|A_{i}|^{\frac{n-2}{n}}\frac{|B_{i,K}|}{|A_{i}||A_{i+K}|}2^{2(i+K)}\int_{B_{i,K}}|\nabla_{g_{C(L)}}u|^{2}.

The volumes are estimated as follows:
|A_{i}|^{\frac{n-2}{n}}\sim 2^{i(n-2)},|B_{i,K}|\sim K\sum_{j=0}^{K}2^{in},(|A_{i}||A_{i+K}|)^{-1}\sim 2^{-ni}2^{-n(i+K)}
, and ordering all the powers of
2
:

2^{i(n-2)}\cdot 2^{-ni}2^{-n(i+K)}\cdot 2^{(i+j)n}\cdot 2^{2i}2^{K}=\text{constant}.

Furthermore,

\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{B_{i,K}}|\nabla_{g_{C(L)}}u|^{2}\leqslant K\int_{C(L)}|\nabla_{g_{C(L)}}u|^{2},

finishing the proof for the metric
g_{C(L)}
.
∎

Proposition A.2
.

Under the same assumptions as in Proposition
A.1
and for all
u\in C_{0}^{\infty}(B_{r}(p))
, then

\left(\int_{B_{r}(p)}u^{2}\dvol_{g}\right)^{1+\frac{2}{n}}\leqslant C\left(\int_{B_{r}(p)}|\nabla_{g}u|^{2}\dvol_{g}\right)\left(\int_{B_{r}(p)}|u|\dvol_{g}\right)^{\frac{4}{n}}.

Proof.

We again assume that
u\geqslant 0
. By Proposition
A.1
and Hölder’s inequality:

\displaystyle\int u^{2}\dvol_{g}

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{r}(p)}u^{a}u^{b}\dvol_{g}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\int_{B_{r}(p)}u^{ap}\dvol_{g}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\left(\int_{B_{r}(p)}u^{bq}\dvol_{g}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle=C\left(\int_{B_{r}(p)}|\nabla_{g}u|^{2}\dvol_{g}\right)^{\frac{n}{p(n-2)}}\left(\int_{B_{r}(p)}u^{bq}\dvol_{g}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}},

with
ap=\frac{2n}{n-2}
,
bq=1
,
a+b=2
, and
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
.
∎

Appendix B
Gradient estimate of the Heat Kernel

The following proposition is known for the Ricci flow as the Bernstein-Bando-Shi estimate
[
16
, Theorem 14.5]
, but we record the statement for the heat equation. See
[
26
, Proposition 2.4]
for the statement on elliptic differential equations.

Proposition B.1
.

Let
(M,g)
be a Riemannian manifold of dimension
n
. Assume that
\Omega^{\prime}\subset\Omega\subset M
are open sets such that
\overline{\Omega^{\prime}}\subset\Omega
and
\Omega
has compact closure in
M
. Let
0<t_{1}<t_{2}<t_{3}<t_{4}
. Assume that there exist Lipschitz continuous functions
\chi\colon M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R},\beta\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\chi\equiv 1
on
\Omega^{\prime}\times[t_{2},t_{3}]
, with support in
\Omega\times[t_{1},t_{4}]
, and such that there exist constants
s>0,C>1
with

(B.1)

s\chi^{-\frac{1}{2}}|\nabla\chi|+s^{2}(\Delta\chi)^{-}+s^{2}|\partial_{t}\chi|\leqslant C,\quad C\Delta\beta\geqslant 1,\quad 0\leqslant\beta<Cs^{2},

where the differential inequalities are to be interpreted in the weak sense. Let
\Rm
be the curvature tensor of
g
, and assume that on
\Omega
:

(B.2)

\sum_{j=0}^{k}s^{j+2}|\nabla^{j}\Rm|\leqslant C.

Furthermore, on
\Omega\times[t_{1},t_{4}]
, assume that
u
is a smooth function satisfying
(\partial_{t}-\Delta)u=0
, and that there is
C_{0}>0
such that

(B.3)

\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}s^{j}|\nabla^{j}u|\leqslant C_{0}.

Then
s^{k}|\nabla^{k}u|\leqslant C^{\prime}C_{0}
on
\Omega^{\prime}\times[t_{2},t_{3}]
, where
C^{\prime}>1
only depends on
k,\Omega
, and
\Omega^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By Bochner’s formula:

(B.4)

(\partial_{t}-\Delta)\frac{1}{2}|\nabla^{k}u|^{2}=-|\nabla^{k+1}u|^{2}-\left\langle[\Delta,\nabla^{k}]u,\nabla^{k}u\right\rangle,

for
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and because
u
solves the heat equation. The constant
C
may change in the proof but is independent of estimates on
u
. Define
\Phi\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}(\chi^{2}|\nabla^{k}u|^{2}+s^{-2}C_{1}|\nabla^{k-1}u|^{2})
. Using (
B.4
), one computes

(B.5)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-\Delta)\Phi=

\displaystyle(\chi(\partial_{t}\chi-\Delta\chi)-|\nabla\chi|^{2}-s^{2}C_{1})|\nabla^{k}u|^{2}-\chi\left\langle\nabla\chi,\nabla|\nabla^{k}u|^{2}\right\rangle-\chi^{2}|\nabla^{k+1}u|^{2}

\displaystyle-\chi^{2}\left\langle[\Delta,\nabla^{k}]u,\nabla^{k}u\right\rangle-C_{1}s^{-2}\left\langle[\Delta,\nabla^{k-1}]u,\nabla^{k-1}u\right\rangle.

By the inequality
|\chi\left\langle\nabla\chi,\nabla|\nabla^{k}u|^{2}\right\rangle|\leqslant\epsilon\chi^{2}|\nabla^{k+1}u|^{2}+\frac{4}{\epsilon}|\nabla\chi|^{2}|\nabla^{k}u|^{2}
for every
\epsilon>0
and the estimates for the commutator
[
3
, Lemma 2]
:

|[\Delta,\nabla^{k}]T|\leqslant C(p+1)\sum_{j=0}^{k}\frac{(k+2)!}{(j+2)!(k-j)!}|\nabla^{j}\Rm||\nabla^{k-j}T|,

where
C
only depends on
\dim M
and
T
is any
p
-tensor, (
B.5
) can be estimated as

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-\Delta)\Phi=

\displaystyle(\chi(\partial_{t}\chi-\Delta\chi)-|\nabla\chi|^{2}-s^{2}C_{1})|\nabla^{k}u|^{2}-\chi\left\langle\nabla\chi,\nabla|\nabla^{k}u|^{2}\right\rangle-\chi^{2}|\nabla^{k+1}u|^{2}

\displaystyle-\chi^{2}\left\langle[\Delta,\nabla^{k}]u,\nabla^{k}u\right\rangle-C_{1}s^{-2}\left\langle[\Delta,\nabla^{k-1}]u,\nabla^{k-1}u\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle(\chi(\partial_{t}\chi-\Delta\chi)-|\nabla\chi|^{2}-s^{-2}C_{1})|\nabla^{k}u|^{2}+\frac{4}{\epsilon}|\nabla\chi|^{2}|\nabla^{k}u|^{2}

\displaystyle+\chi^{2}C|\nabla^{k}u|\sum_{j=0}^{k}|\nabla^{j}\Rm||\nabla^{k-j}u|+C_{1}s^{-2}C|\nabla^{k-1}u|\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}|\nabla^{j}\Rm||\nabla^{k-j-1}u|

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle(\chi(\partial_{t}\chi-\Delta\chi)-|\nabla\chi|^{2}-s^{-2}C_{1})|\nabla^{k}u|^{2}+\frac{4}{\epsilon}|\nabla\chi|^{2}|\nabla^{k}u|^{2}

\displaystyle+\chi^{2}CC_{0}|\nabla^{k}u|\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}s^{-2-j}s^{-k+j}+C_{1}C_{0}s^{-2}Cs^{-k+1}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}s^{-j-2}s^{-k+j+1}

\displaystyle+\chi^{2}Cs^{-2}|\nabla^{k}u|^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle(\chi(\partial_{t}\chi-\Delta\chi)-|\nabla\chi|^{2}-s^{-2}C_{1})|\nabla^{k}u|^{2}+\frac{4}{\epsilon}|\nabla\chi|^{2}|\nabla^{k}u|^{2}

\displaystyle+\chi^{2}CC_{0}s^{-2k-2}|\nabla^{k}u|+CC_{1}C_{0}s^{-2k-2}+\chi^{2}Cs^{-2}|\nabla^{k}u|^{2}+\chi^{2}Cs^{-2}|\nabla^{k}u|^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle(\chi(\partial_{t}\chi-\Delta\chi)-|\nabla\chi|^{2}-s^{-2}C_{1})|\nabla^{k}u|^{2}+\frac{4}{\epsilon}|\nabla\chi|^{2}|\nabla^{k}u|^{2}

\displaystyle+\chi^{2}CC_{0}s^{-2k-2}+\chi^{2}CC_{0}s^{-2}|\nabla^{k}u|^{2}+CC_{1}C_{0}s^{-2k-2}+\chi^{2}Cs^{-2}|\nabla^{k}u|^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle(\chi(\partial_{t}\chi-\Delta\chi)-|\nabla\chi|^{2}+\chi^{2}CC_{0}+\chi^{2}Cs^{-2}+\frac{4}{\epsilon}|\nabla\chi|^{2}-s^{-2}C_{1})|\nabla^{k}u|^{2}

\displaystyle+(\chi^{2}+C_{1})CC_{0}s^{-2k-2},

where we have estimated
|\nabla^{j}\Rm||\nabla^{k-j}u|
via (
B.2
) and (
B.3
). By choosing
C_{1}\geqslant 1
big enough, the first parenthesis above is nonpositive, and hence

(\partial_{t}-\Delta)\Phi\leqslant CC_{1}C_{0}s^{-2k-2}.

By the construction of
\beta
, then

(\partial_{t}-\Delta)(\Phi-CC_{1}C_{0}s^{-2k-2}\beta)\leqslant 0.

Using the maximum principle for parabolic partial differential equations, it follows that

\sup_{\Omega^{\prime}\times[t_{2},t_{3}]}|\nabla^{k}u|\leqslant Cs^{-k}.

∎

Appendix C
Harmonic Functions and Constant-Trace Forms of Prescribed Growth

Throughout this section,
(C(L),g_{C(L)})
denotes a
n
-dimensional Riemannian conical manifold (not necessarily Ricci-flat) of dimension
n\geqslant 3
. For Lemma
C.4
and Proposition
C.5
, we need to assume that
(C(L),\omega_{C(L)})
is Ricci-flat and Kähler. We show that harmonic functions and constant trace-forms of growth
\mathcal{O}(r^{\beta})
as
r\to 0
and
\mathcal{O}(r^{\alpha})
as
r\to\infty
for
\beta\leqslant\alpha
satisfy nice regularity properties. On
{C(L)}\cong\mathbb{R}_{+}\times L
with coordinates
(r,x)
, define for
\beta\leqslant\alpha
the function
\theta_{\beta,\alpha}\colon{C(L)}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
by

(C.1)

\theta_{\beta,\alpha}(r,x)=\begin{cases}r^{\beta}&r\leqslant 1,\\
r^{\alpha}&r>1.\end{cases}

The estimation constants may change in all lines below. Denote by
\Delta_{L}
the Laplacian of
g_{C(L)}|_{\{r=1\}}
and by
\dvol_{L}\coloneqq\dvol_{g_{C(L)}|_{\{r=1\}}}
the volume form on the link. On the link
L\cong\{r=1\}
with Laplacian
\Delta_{L}
, let
(\varphi_{j})
be an orthonormal basis of eigenfunctions of
-\Delta_{L}
with eigenvalues
0=\lambda_{0}<\lambda_{1}\leqslant\cdots
, counted with multiplicities.

Proposition C.1
.

Let
f\colon{C(L)}\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth function such that
\Delta_{g_{{C(L)}}}f=0
and
|f|\leqslant C\theta_{\beta,\alpha}
. Then
f
splits into a finite sum

f=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(a_{j}^{+}r^{c_{j}^{+}}+a_{j}^{-}r^{c_{j}^{-}})\varphi_{j},\quad c_{j}^{+}\geqslant 0,c_{j}^{-}\leqslant 0,

where
a_{j}^{\pm}\neq 0
only if
c_{j}^{\pm}\in[\beta,\alpha]
.

If
(C(L),g_{C(L)})
has nonnegative Ricci curvature, then
c_{j}^{-}\leqslant 1-n
and
c_{j}^{+}\geqslant 1
for
j\geqslant 1
with equality if and only if
(C(L)\cup\{o\},g_{C(L)})\cong(\mathbb{R}^{n},g_{\eucl})
.

Proof.

Let
f\colon{C(L)}\to\mathbb{R}
be harmonic with respect to
\Delta_{g_{{C(L)}}}
. On the link
L=\{r=1\}
with Laplacian
\Delta_{L}
, let
(\varphi_{j})
be an orthonormal basis of eigenfunctions of
-\Delta_{L}
with eigenvalues
0=\lambda_{0}<\lambda_{1}\leqslant\cdots
. Expand
f
in terms of these functions:

f=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}g_{j}(r)\varphi_{j}(x),

with the sum converging in
L^{2}_{\loc}({C(L)})
. As
f
is smooth, the convergence can be upgraded to
W^{2,k}_{\loc}({C(L)})
for any
k\geqslant 0
, and hence also to
C^{\infty}_{\loc}({C(L)})
by the Morrey embedding. The full Laplacian
\Delta_{g_{{C(L)}}}
is:

(C.2)

\Delta_{g_{{C(L)}}}f=\partial_{r}^{2}f+\frac{n-1}{r}\partial_{r}f+\Delta_{L}f.

We solve (
C.2
) for each eigenfunction
\varphi_{j}
, and it follows that:

(C.3)

f=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(a_{j}^{+}r^{c_{j}^{+}}+a_{j}^{-}r^{c_{j}^{-}})\varphi_{j},

with

(C.4)

c_{j}^{\pm}=\frac{2-n\pm\sqrt{(n-2)^{2}+4\lambda_{j}}}{2}.

Denote by
\left\langle f,\varphi_{j}\right\rangle_{L}
the
L^{2}
-inner product of
f
and
\varphi_{j}
at every level set
\{r=R\}
with the volume form at
\{r=1\}
:

(C.5)

\left\langle f,\varphi_{j}\right\rangle_{L}(R)=\int_{\{r=R\}}f(r,x)\varphi_{j}(x)\dvol_{L}(x).

Then

|a_{j}^{+}r^{c_{j}^{+}}+a_{j}^{-}r^{c_{j}^{-}}|=|\left\langle f,\varphi_{j}\right\rangle_{L}|\leqslant|\left\langle f,f\right\rangle_{L}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\leqslant C\theta_{\beta,\alpha}(r).

Taking
r\to 0
or
r\to\infty
, we see that
a_{j}^{\pm}\neq 0
only if
c_{j}^{\pm}\in[\beta,\alpha]
.

If
(C(L),g_{C(L)})
has nonnegative Ricci curvature, then
(L,g_{L})
has Ricci curvature
(n-2)
. The Lichnerowicz-Obata Theorem
[
30
, Theorem 2.1]
shows that
\lambda_{1}\geqslant n-1
with equality if and only if
(C(L)\cup\{o\},g_{C(L)})\cong(\mathbb{R}^{n},g_{\eucl})
. Substituting this result into (
C.4
) shows that
c_{j}^{+}\geqslant 1
with equality if and only if
(C(L)\cup\{o\},g_{C(L)})\cong(\mathbb{R}^{2m},g_{\eucl})
. The same argument shows
c_{j}^{-}\leqslant 1-n
.
∎

A similar statement holds for exact 2-forms of constant trace in Kähler case. To prove this, we need the following two lemmas:

Lemma C.2

(Poincaré Lemma with Estimates)
.

Let
\eta\in\Omega^{2}({C(L)})
satisfy
d\eta=0
and

|\nabla_{g_{C(L)}}^{l}\eta|\leqslant Cr^{-l}\theta_{\beta,\alpha}(r),\quad l=0,\dots,k,

for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
, constant
C>0
, and any
\beta>-2
. Then there exists a form
\zeta\in\Omega^{1}({C(L)})
such that
\eta=d\zeta
and with the estimates:

|\nabla_{g_{C(L)}}^{l}\zeta|\leqslant C^{\prime}r^{1-l}\theta_{\beta,\alpha}(r),\quad l=0,\dots,k,

for
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime}(C,k,\alpha)>0
.

Proof.

The proof mimics the classical proof of the Poincaré Lemma using the Lie derivative. Let
\Theta_{t}\colon{C(L)}\to{C(L)}
denote the one-parameter family of scaling maps
p=(r^{\prime},x)\mapsto(e^{t}\cdot r^{\prime},x)
with generating vector field
r\partial_{r}
. Then

\frac{d}{dt}\Theta_{t}^{*}\eta=\Theta_{t}^{*}\mathscr{L}_{r\partial_{r}}\eta.

Integrating both sides and using the Cartan formula:

(C.6)

\displaystyle\Theta_{t_{1}}^{*}\eta-\Theta_{t_{0}}^{*}\eta

\displaystyle=d\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\Theta_{t}^{*}i(r\partial_{r})\eta\,dt+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\Theta_{t}^{*}i(r\partial_{r})d\eta\,dt

\displaystyle=d\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\Theta_{t}^{*}i(r\partial_{r})\eta\,dt,

as
d\eta=0
. Write
r=e^{t}
and
p=(e^{t^{\prime}},x)
. We estimate the integrand by

|\Theta_{t}^{*}i(r\partial_{r})\eta_{(e^{t^{\prime}},x)}|\leqslant e^{t}\Theta_{t}^{*}|i(r\partial_{r})\eta_{(e^{t^{\prime}},x)}|\leqslant e^{t}\Theta_{t}^{*}(|r\partial_{r}||\eta_{(e^{t^{\prime}},x)}|)\leqslant Ce^{2t+t^{\prime}}\theta_{\beta,\alpha}(e^{t+t^{\prime}}).

For any fixed
t_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\beta>-2
ensures that the limit of (
C.6
) exists as
t_{0}\to-\infty
. Hence, setting
t_{1}=0
and
t_{0}\to-\infty
, we find:

\eta=d\int_{-\infty}^{0}\Theta_{t}^{*}i(r\partial_{r})\eta\,dt\eqqcolon d\zeta.

By using the Koszul formula, we see that
\nabla_{g_{C(L)}}r\partial_{r}=\Id
regarded as a map
\mathfrak{X}({C(L)})\to\mathfrak{X}({C(L)})
. Hence
\nabla_{g_{C(L)}}^{l}r\partial_{r}=0
for
l\geqslant 2
and
|\nabla_{g_{C(L)}}r\partial_{r}|=1
. Therefore, we estimate
\zeta
and its derivatives using the product rule:

(C.7)

\displaystyle|\nabla_{g_{C(L)}}^{l}\zeta|_{(e^{t^{\prime}},x)}

\displaystyle=\left|\nabla_{g_{C(L)}}^{l}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\Theta_{t}^{*}i(r\partial_{r})\eta_{(e^{t^{\prime}},x)}\,dt\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{-\infty}^{0}\left|\nabla_{g_{C(L)}}^{l}\left(\Theta_{t}^{*}i(r\partial_{r})\eta_{(e^{t^{\prime}},x)}\right)\right|\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\left|\Theta_{t}^{*}\left(\nabla_{(\Theta_{t}^{-1})^{*}g_{C(L)}}^{l}\left(i(r\partial_{r})\eta_{(e^{t^{\prime}},x)}\right)\right)\right|\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\left|\Theta_{t}^{*}\left(\nabla_{g_{C(L)}}^{l}\left(i(r\partial_{r})\eta_{(e^{t^{\prime}},x)}\right)\right)\right|\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{(l+1)t}\Theta_{t}^{*}\left|\nabla_{g_{C(L)}}^{l}(i(r\partial_{r})\eta)\right|\,dt

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{-\infty}^{0}Ce^{(1+l)t}e^{(1-l)(t^{\prime}+t)}\theta_{\beta,\alpha}(e^{t^{\prime}+t})\,dt

\displaystyle=C\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{(1-l)t^{\prime}}e^{2t}\theta_{\beta,\alpha}(e^{t^{\prime}+t})\,dt

\displaystyle=C\begin{cases}\frac{e^{(1+\beta-l)t^{\prime}}}{2+\beta}&t^{\prime}\leqslant 0,\\
\left(\frac{e^{(-1-l)t^{\prime}}}{2+\beta}-\frac{e^{(-1-l)t^{\prime}}}{2+\alpha}\right)+\frac{e^{(1+\alpha-l)t^{\prime}}}{2+\alpha}&t^{\prime}>0,\end{cases}

for
l=0,\dots,k
. These are exactly the desired estimates.
∎

For the next lemma, we need the following definition: Let
\mathcal{E}=\{c_{j}^{\pm}\in\mathbb{R}\mid\text{$c_{j}^{\pm}$ is a solution of \eqref{ExpressionCi}}\}
, i.e. the set of homogeneity factors of harmonic functions on
(C(L),g_{C(L)})
.

Lemma C.3
.

Take
\beta\leqslant 0<\alpha
and such that
2+\alpha,2+\beta\notin\mathcal{E}
. If
f\colon{C(L)}\to\mathbb{R}
is a smooth function satisfying

|\nabla^{l}f|\leqslant Cr^{-l}\theta_{\beta,\alpha}(r),\quad l=0,\dots,k,

for some
C>0
,
k\geqslant 3n+1
,
\beta\leqslant 0<\alpha
, then there exists a smooth function
u\colon{C(L)}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

\Delta_{g_{C(L)}}u=f,\qquad|\nabla^{l}u|\leqslant C^{\prime}r^{2-l}\theta_{\beta,\alpha}(r),\quad l=0,\dots,k+2,

for some constant
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime}(C,k,\alpha,\beta)>0
.

Proof.

As in the proof of Proposition
C.1
, pick an orthonormal basis of eigenfunctions
(\varphi_{j})
of
-\Delta_{L}
with eigenvalues
0=\lambda_{0}<\lambda_{1}\leqslant\cdots
. On
{C(L)}\cong\mathbb{R}_{+}\times L
with coordinates
(r,x)
, expand
f
in terms of these functions:

f=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}g_{j}(r)\varphi_{j}(x),

with the sum converging in
L^{2}_{\loc}({C(L)})
. As
f
is smooth, the convergence can be upgraded to
W^{2,k}_{\loc}({C(L)})
for any
k\geqslant 0
, and hence also to
C^{\infty}_{\loc}({C(L)})
by the Morrey embedding. Let
\left\langle f,\varphi_{j}\right\rangle_{L}
be as in (
C.5
). The growth property of
f
implies that

(C.8)

|g_{j}|=|\left\langle f,\varphi_{j}\right\rangle_{L}|\leqslant C\theta_{\beta,\alpha}(r).

We seek a solution
u
of the same form:

u=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}h_{j}(r)\varphi_{j}(x).

Write
\Delta_{g_{C(L)}}u=\partial_{r}^{2}u+\frac{n-1}{r}\partial_{r}u+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Delta_{L}u
. We apply the Laplacian on each
h_{j}(r)\varphi_{j}(x)
to obtain an equation of the form:

(C.9)

(\partial_{r}^{2}h_{j}+\frac{n-1}{r}\partial_{r}h_{j}-\frac{\lambda_{j}}{r^{2}}h_{j})\varphi_{j}(x)=g_{j}(r)\varphi_{j}(x).

The homogeneous solutions are
r^{c_{j}^{+}}
and
r^{c_{j}^{-}}
with

(C.10)

c_{j}^{\pm}=\frac{2-n\pm\sqrt{(n-2)^{2}+4\lambda_{j}}}{2}.

To solve for the inhomogeneous part, the Wronskian is:

W_{j}(r)=\begin{vmatrix}r^{c_{j}^{+}}&r^{c_{j}^{-}}\\
c_{j}^{+}r^{c_{j}^{+}-1}&c_{j}^{-}r^{c_{j}^{-}-1}\end{vmatrix},

giving the general solution of (
C.9
):

h_{j}=\hat{h}_{j}+\Lambda_{j}^{+}r^{c_{j}^{+}}+\Lambda_{j}^{-}r^{c_{j}^{-}},

with

(C.11)

\hat{h}_{j}=\frac{1}{c_{j}^{-}-c_{j}^{+}}\left(-r^{c_{j}^{+}}\int_{1}^{r}s^{1-c_{j}^{+}}g_{j}(s)\,ds+r^{c_{j}^{-}}\int_{1}^{r}s^{1-c_{j}^{-}}g_{j}(s)\,ds\right).

We need to fix
\Lambda_{j}^{\pm}
such that
h_{j}
has the desired asymptotics. Set

\Lambda_{j}^{+}\coloneqq\begin{cases}0&c_{j}^{+}<2+\alpha,\\
\frac{1}{c_{j}^{-}-c_{j}^{+}}\left(r^{c_{j}^{+}}\int_{1}^{\infty}s^{1-c_{j}^{+}}g_{j}(s)\,ds\right)&c_{j}^{+}>2+\alpha,\end{cases}

and

\Lambda_{j}^{-}\coloneqq\begin{cases}0&c_{j}^{-}>2+\beta,\\
\frac{-1}{c_{j}^{-}-c_{j}^{+}}\left(r^{c_{j}^{-}}\int_{1}^{0}s^{1-c_{j}^{-}}g_{j}(s)\,ds\right)&c_{j}^{-}<2+\beta,\end{cases}

2+\alpha\neq c_{j}^{+}
and
2+\beta\neq c_{j}^{-}
by assumption.
The integrals of
\Lambda_{j}^{\pm}
exist by the asymptotics of
g_{j}
. With these choices of
\Lambda_{j}^{\pm}
, it follows that

|h_{j}|\leqslant C\theta_{\beta,\alpha}(r).

We show the the proof for
\Lambda_{j}^{+}
. If
c_{j}^{+}<2+\alpha
, then

\displaystyle\left|r^{c_{j}^{+}}\int_{1}^{r}s^{1-c_{j}^{+}}g_{j}(s)\,ds\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\left|Cr^{c_{j}^{+}}\int_{1}^{r}s^{1-c_{j}^{+}+\alpha}\right|

\displaystyle=Cr^{c_{j}^{+}}\left|\left[\frac{s^{2-c_{j}^{+}+\alpha}}{2-c_{j}^{+}+\alpha}\right]_{1}^{r}\right|

\displaystyle=C\left|\frac{r^{2+\alpha}-r^{c_{j}^{+}}}{2-c_{j}^{+}+\alpha}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant Cr^{2}\theta_{\beta,\alpha}(r).

For
c_{j}^{+}>2+\alpha
, then

\displaystyle\left|-r^{c_{j}^{+}}\int_{1}^{r}s^{1-c_{j}^{+}}g_{j}(s)\,ds+r^{c_{j}^{+}}\int_{1}^{\infty}s^{1-c_{j}^{+}}g_{j}(s)\,ds\right|

\displaystyle=\left|r^{c_{j}^{+}}\int_{r}^{\infty}s^{1-c_{j}^{+}}g_{j}(s)\,ds\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant Cr^{c_{j}^{+}}\left|\left[\frac{s^{2-c_{j}^{+}+\alpha}}{2-c_{j}^{+}+\alpha}\right]_{r}^{\infty}\right|

\displaystyle=C\frac{-r^{2+\alpha}}{2-c_{j}^{+}+\alpha}\leqslant C\theta_{\beta,\alpha}(r).

Next, we bound
h_{j}
in terms of
g_{j}
and the associated eigenvalue of
\varphi_{j}
. First, for
j\neq 0
:

(C.12)

\displaystyle|g_{j}(r)|

\displaystyle=\lambda_{j}^{-k}\left|\left\langle f,\Delta_{L}^{k}\varphi_{j}\right\rangle_{L}\right|

\displaystyle=\lambda_{j}^{-k}\left|\left\langle\Delta_{L}^{k}f,\varphi_{j}\right\rangle_{L}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant C\lambda_{j}^{-k}r^{2k}\left|\left\langle\Delta_{g_{{C(L)}}}^{k}f,\Delta_{{g_{{C(L)}}}}^{k}f\right\rangle_{L}\right|^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\lambda_{j}^{-k}\theta_{\beta,\alpha}(r),

for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
. By (
C.11
) and (
C.12
), there exists a constant
C>0
such that

|h_{j}(r)|+r|h_{j}^{\prime}(r)|+r^{2}|h_{j}^{\prime\prime}(r)|\leqslant C\lambda_{j}^{-k}r^{2}\theta_{\beta,\alpha}(r).

The expression

(C.13)

u=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}h_{j}(r)\varphi_{j}(x)

now formally satisfies
\Delta_{g_{C(L)}}u=f
. To show that
u
is an actual solution, we need to show that the sum (
C.13
) converges in
C^{2}_{\loc}({C(L)})
. The Morrey embedding
[
33
, 10. Theorem]
shows that
\left\lVert\varphi_{j}\right\rVert_{2l-\frac{n}{2},L}\leqslant C\left\lVert\varphi_{j}\right\rVert_{W^{2l,2}(L)}
for
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. As
-\Delta_{L}\varphi_{j}=\lambda_{j}\varphi_{j}
and
\left\lVert\varphi_{j}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\{r=1\})}=1
we obtain:

\left\lVert\varphi_{j}\right\rVert_{2,L}\leqslant C(1+\lambda_{j})^{l}.

for some constant
C>0
depending only on
(C(L),g_{C(L)})
and
l\geqslant 1+\frac{n}{4}
a natural number.

By Weyl’s Asymptotic Formula
[
8
, p. 155]
,
\lambda_{j}\sim j^{\frac{1}{2n}}
, so

(C.14)

\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}|\nabla_{g_{{C(L)}}}^{l}(h_{j}(r)\varphi_{j}(x))|\leqslant C\theta_{\beta,\alpha}(r)r^{2-k}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}j^{\frac{-k+l}{2n}},\quad l=0,1,2.

Choose
k
such that
\frac{-k+l}{2n}<-1
, and the sum (
C.13
) converges in
C^{2}_{\loc}({C(L)})
. For this, it is sufficient to choose
k=3n+1
. Smoothness of
u
follows by elliptic regularity, and the growth property of
\nabla_{g_{{C(L)}}}^{l}u
follows from (
C.14
) for
l=0,1,2
. Growth of the higher order terms follow by the Schauder estimates and rescaling.
∎

Lemma C.4
.

On the Ricci-flat Kähler cone
(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
, let
\eta\in\Omega^{1}(L)
be a real, co-closed 1-form such that
r^{2}\eta
is harmonic on
(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
and the dual vector field
\eta^{\sharp}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathscr{C})
is a Killing field on
(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
. Then there exists a 2-homogeneous,
\xi
-invariant function
q\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathbb{R}
with constant Laplacian such that

\partial(r^{2}\eta)^{(0,1)}=i\partial\overline{\partial}q.

Proof.

First, if
(\mathscr{C}\cup\{o\},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})\cong(\mathbb{C}^{m},\omega_{\eucl})
, then
\partial(r^{2}\eta)^{(0,1)}
is a
2
-homogeneous harmonic form on
(\mathbb{C}^{m},\omega_{\eucl})
.
\partial(r^{2}\eta)^{(0,1)}
is therefore a constant
(1,1)
-form, and the lemma is trivial.

Assuming
(\mathscr{C}\cup\{o\},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})\neq(\mathbb{C}^{m},\omega_{\eucl})
, let
g_{\mathscr{C}}
be the Riemannian metric associated to
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
, and extend to a
\mathbb{C}
-bilinear form on the complexified tangent bundle
T_{\mathbb{C}}\mathscr{C}
. By Tanno
[
47
, Theorem 4.4]
, if
(\mathscr{C},g_{\mathscr{C}})
does not admit a hyperkähler structure, then all Killing vector fields are holomorphic. Since the dual vector field
(r^{2}\eta)^{\sharp}
is a homogeneous Killing field of linear growth on
\mathscr{C}
, it commutes with the scaling vector field
r\partial_{r}
(see the proof of Theorem
2.26
):

\left[(r^{2}\eta)^{\sharp},r\partial_{r}\right]=\mathscr{L}_{r\partial_{r}}(r^{2}\eta)^{\sharp}=(1-1)(r^{2}\eta)^{\sharp}=0.

By
[
28
, Theorem 2.14]
(or see Theorem
2.26
), the Killing field
(r^{2}\eta)^{\sharp}
can be expressed as

(r^{2}\eta)^{\sharp}=J\nabla q,

for some
\xi
-invariant, 2-homogeneous function
q
of constant Laplacian. Therefore:

\partial(r^{2}\eta)^{(0,1)}=-\partial(d^{c}q)^{(0,1)}=-i\partial\overline{\partial}q.

Now consider the case where
(\mathscr{C},g_{\mathscr{C}})
is hyperkähler, with complex structures
I
,
J
, and
K
, where
I
corresponds to the already given complex structure on
\mathscr{C}
. Suppose
(r^{2}\eta)^{\sharp}
is not holomorphic. Then, up to linear combination and dropping the holomorphic part, we must have
(r^{2}\eta)^{\sharp}=J(r\partial_{r})
or
(r^{2}\eta)^{\sharp}=K(r\partial_{r})
by
[
47
, Theorem 4.4]
. Assume
(r^{2}\eta)^{\sharp}=J(r\partial_{r})
. Then:

r^{2}\eta=(J(r\partial_{r}))^{\flat}=g_{\mathscr{C}}(J(r\partial_{r}),\cdot),

and its
(0,1)
-part is given by:

\displaystyle(r^{2}\eta)^{(0,1)}

\displaystyle=g_{\mathscr{C}}(J(r\partial_{r}),\cdot)+ig_{\mathscr{C}}(J(r\partial_{r}),I\cdot)

\displaystyle=g_{\mathscr{C}}(J(r\partial_{r})-iIJ(r\partial_{r}),\cdot)

\displaystyle=g_{\mathscr{C}}(J(r\partial_{r}+iI(r\partial_{r})),\cdot).

Define the vector field:

W=r\partial_{r}+iI(r\partial_{r})\in\Gamma(T^{0,1}\mathscr{C}).

Now compute
\partial(r^{2}\eta)^{(0,1)}
for
X\in\Gamma(T^{1,0}\mathscr{C})
and
Y\in\Gamma(T^{0,1}\mathscr{C})
:

\displaystyle(\partial(r^{2}\eta^{(0,1)}))(X,Y)

\displaystyle=(d(r^{2}\eta^{(0,1)}))(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{X}(r^{2}\eta^{(0,1)})(Y)-\nabla_{Y}(r^{2}\eta^{(0,1)})(X)

\displaystyle=g_{\mathscr{C}}(\nabla_{X}JW,Y)+g_{\mathscr{C}}(\nabla_{Y}JW,X).

As
J\colon T^{1,0}\mathscr{C}\to T^{0,1}\mathscr{C}
and
J\colon T^{0,1}\mathscr{C}\to T^{1,0}\mathscr{C}
, and
\nabla_{Z}
preserves the decomposition
T_{\mathbb{C}}\mathscr{C}=T^{1,0}\mathscr{C}\oplus T^{0,1}\mathscr{C}
for any vector field
Z
, then
\nabla_{Y}JW,X\in\Gamma(T^{1,0}\mathscr{C})
. As
g_{\mathscr{C}}
is the bilinear extension of the metric on
T\mathscr{C}
, it follows that
g_{\mathscr{C}}(\nabla_{Y}JW,X)=0
. Hence, using compatibility with
J
:

(\partial(r^{2}\eta^{(0,1)}))(X,Y)=g_{\mathscr{C}}(\nabla_{X}JW,Y)=-g_{\mathscr{C}}(\nabla_{X}W,JY).

Decomposing
W
into real components, we find:

\displaystyle(\partial(r^{2}\eta)^{(0,1)})(X,Y)

\displaystyle=-g_{\mathscr{C}}(\nabla_{X}r\partial_{r},JY)+ig_{\mathscr{C}}(\nabla_{X}r\partial_{r},IJY)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mathscr{C}}(\nabla_{X}\nabla r^{2},JY)+\frac{i}{2}g_{\mathscr{C}}(\nabla_{X}\nabla r^{2},KY)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\Hess(r^{2})(X,JY)+\frac{i}{2}\Hess(r^{2})(X,KY).

The Hessian satisfies:

(C.15)

\Hess(r^{2})(U,V)=2g_{\mathscr{C}}(U,V)

for any
U,V\in\Gamma(T\mathscr{C})
. Decomposing
X
and
Y
into real and imaginary components, using (
C.15
), and substituting back, we obtain:

\displaystyle(\partial(r^{2}\eta)^{(0,1)})(X,Y)

\displaystyle=-g_{\mathscr{C}}(X,JY)+ig_{\mathscr{C}}(X,KY).

However, since
X,JY,KY\in\Gamma(T^{1,0}\mathscr{C})
and
g_{\mathscr{C}}
is the bilinear extension of the metric on
T\mathscr{C}
, then:

(\partial(r^{2}\eta)^{(0,1)})(X,Y)=-g_{\mathscr{C}}(X,JY)+ig_{\mathscr{C}}(X,KY)=0.

∎

Proposition C.5
.

Pick
\beta\leqslant 0<\alpha
such that
\beta\in(-1,0]
and
\alpha>0
is small. Given a Ricci-flat Kähler cone
(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
of complex dimension
m\geqslant 2
, if
\eta\in\Omega^{2}({\mathscr{C}})
is a closed
(1,1)
-form satisfying
\tr_{\omega_{{\mathscr{C}}}}\eta=A
for some constant
A
, and
|\eta|\leqslant C\theta_{\beta,\alpha}
, then

(C.16)

\eta=i\partial\overline{\partial}\left(\frac{A}{2m}r^{2}+r^{2}\varphi\right),

where
r^{2}\varphi
is harmonic and
\varphi
is an eigenfunction on
L
.

Proof.

Pick
\alpha
so small such that
(2,2+\alpha]\cap\mathcal{E}=\emptyset
. If
\beta\in E
, decrease
\beta
such that no new elements of
\mathcal{E}
enter
[\beta,\alpha]
. The first step is to obtain estimates on the derivatives of
\eta
. Pick a point
p\in{\mathscr{C}}
and let
R=\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(o,p)
. Let
\tilde{p}\coloneqq\Phi^{*}(p)
with
\dist_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}(0,\tilde{p})=1
. Pulling
\eta
back by
\Phi_{R}
, then by
[
11
, Lemma 2.1]
, for
\epsilon
small enough, there exists a function
v\in C^{2,\alpha}(B_{\epsilon}(\tilde{p}))
such that

i\partial\overline{\partial}v=\eta,\quad\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{0,B_{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}(\tilde{p})}\leqslant C\left\lVert\Phi_{R}^{*}\eta\right\rVert_{0,B_{\epsilon}(\tilde{p})},

where the constant
C
only depends on
\epsilon
. Taking the trace, we find
\Delta_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}v=A
, and bootstrapping the Schauder estimates shows that

\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{k+2,\alpha,B_{\frac{\epsilon}{4}}(\tilde{p})}\leqslant C_{k}\left\lVert\Phi_{R}^{*}\eta\right\rVert_{0,B_{\epsilon}(\tilde{p})},

for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Hence,

\left\lVert\Phi_{R}^{*}\eta\right\rVert_{k,B_{\frac{\epsilon}{4}}(\tilde{p})}\leqslant C_{k}\left\lVert\Phi_{R}^{*}\eta\right\rVert_{0,B_{\epsilon}(\tilde{p})}.

Pull back by
\Phi_{R^{-1}}
to conclude that

|\nabla_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}^{k}\eta|\leqslant C_{k}r^{-k}|\eta|\leqslant Cr^{-k}\theta_{\beta,\alpha}(r)

for some constant
C>0
and all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Given the estimate on
\nabla_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}\eta
, by Lemma
C.2
, there exists a 1-form
\zeta=\zeta^{1,0}+\zeta^{(0,1)}\in\Omega^{1,0}({\mathscr{C}})\oplus\Omega^{0,1}({\mathscr{C}})
such that
d\zeta=\eta
and
|\nabla_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}^{k}\zeta|\leqslant C_{k}r^{1-k}\theta_{\beta,\alpha}(r)
. By Lemma
C.3
there exists a function
\upsilon
such that
\Delta_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}\upsilon=\overline{\partial}^{*}\zeta^{0,1}
satisfying
|\nabla_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}^{k}\upsilon|\leqslant C_{k}r^{2-k}\theta_{\beta,\alpha}(r)
. Consider

\xi\coloneqq\overline{\partial}\upsilon-\zeta^{(0,1)}.

\overline{\partial}\xi=\overline{\partial}^{*}\xi=0
and so
\Delta_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}\xi=0
by the Kähler identities. By decomposing the Laplacian acting on harmonic 1-forms as done in the proofs of Lemma
2.25
and Lemma
2.26
(for more details, see
[
28
, Lemma B.1]
, more specifically p. 122) and picking
\alpha>0
small enough, there exists a function
\chi
such that
\xi=d\chi+r^{2}\hat{\xi}
and
|\nabla_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}^{k}\chi|\leqslant C_{k}r^{2-k}\theta_{\beta,\alpha}(r)
for
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
r^{2}\hat{\xi}
satisfies the properties of Lemma
C.4
. If
\beta
was very negative, or
\alpha>0
was not assumed to be very small, terms of the form
r^{\tau}\eta^{\prime}
for
\eta^{\prime}
co-closed and
\tau\neq 0
could appear. These terms do not necessarily satisfy Lemma
C.4
. Ricci-flatness of
\omega_{\mathscr{C}}
ensures that the term satisfying
\tau=2
satisfies Lemma
C.4
, i.e.

\partial(r^{2}\hat{\xi})^{(0,1)}=i\partial\overline{\partial}q,

for some 2-homogeneous function
q
with constant Laplacian.

Define:

\psi\coloneqq-i(\upsilon-\chi-iq),

then

\displaystyle i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi

\displaystyle=\partial\left(\overline{\partial}\upsilon-\overline{\partial}\upsilon+\zeta^{(0,1)}+(r^{2}\hat{\xi})^{(0,1)}-i\overline{\partial}q\right)

\displaystyle=\partial\zeta^{(0,1)}+i\partial\overline{\partial}q-i\partial\overline{\partial}q

\displaystyle=\partial\zeta^{(0,1)}.

We conclude that

i\partial\overline{\partial}\,2\re(\psi)=i\partial\overline{\partial}(\psi+\overline{\psi})=\partial\zeta^{(0,1)}+\overline{\partial}\zeta^{1,0}=d\zeta=\eta,

so
2\re(\psi)
is a potential of
\eta
satisfying
|\nabla_{\omega_{\mathscr{C}}}^{k}2\re(\psi)|\leqslant C_{k}^{\prime}r^{-k}\theta_{\beta,\alpha}(r)
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

To show that
\eta
is actually 2-homogeneous, note that by assumption:

\tr_{\omega_{{\mathscr{C}}}}\eta=\tr_{\omega_{{\mathscr{C}}}}i\partial\overline{\partial}\,2\re(\psi)=\Delta_{\omega_{{\mathscr{C}}}}2\re(\psi)=A,

for some constant
A\in\mathbb{R}
. Define
\tilde{\psi}=2\re(\psi)-A\frac{r^{2}}{2m}
, then
\Delta_{\omega_{{\mathscr{C}}}}\tilde{\psi}=0
. We conclude that
\tilde{\psi}
has as expansion as given in Proposition
C.1
. If
c_{j}^{\pm}\in(1,2)
, then
r^{c_{j}^{\pm}}\varphi_{j}
is a pluriharmonic function by
[
28
, Theorem 2.14]
and as
(\mathscr{C},\omega_{\mathscr{C}})
is Ricci-flat.
∎
